# Part I: The Basic Concepts of Chemistry

## Chapter 1: What Is Chemistry, and Why Do I Need to Know Some?

### Understanding What Chemistry Is
Chemistry is the branch of science that studies the composition, structure, properties, and changes of matter. It is often referred to as the "central science" because it connects physics with other natural sciences such as geology and biology.

### Distinguishing between Science and Technology
Science is the systematic study of the natural world through observation and experimentation, while technology is the application of scientific knowledge for practical purposes. Chemistry plays a crucial role in developing new materials, medicines, and energy sources.

### Deciphering the Scientific Method
The scientific method is a systematic approach to research and experimentation. It typically involves the following steps:
1. **Observation**: Gathering data and noticing phenomena.
2. **Question**: Formulating a question based on observations.
3. **Hypothesis**: Proposing a testable explanation for the observations.
4. **Experimentation**: Conducting experiments to test the hypothesis.
5. **Analysis**: Analyzing the data collected from experiments.
6. **Conclusion**: Drawing conclusions based on the analysis and determining whether the hypothesis is supported or refuted.
7. **Communication**: Sharing results with the scientific community for further validation and exploration.
```

# Knowing How to Handle Significant Figures

## Comparing numbers: Exact and counted versus measured
- Exact numbers have an infinite number of significant figures (e.g., 100 apples).
- Measured numbers have a limited number of significant figures based on the precision of the measurement tool (e.g., 3.14 m).

## Determining the number of significant figures in a measured number
1. Non-zero digits are always significant.
2. Any zeros between significant digits are significant.
3. Leading zeros are not significant.
4. Trailing zeros in a decimal number are significant.
5. In a whole number without a decimal point, trailing zeros may or may not be significant.

## Reporting the correct number of significant figures
- When performing calculations, the result should be reported with the same number of significant figures as the measurement with the least number of significant figures.

## Rounding off numbers
- If the digit to be removed is less than 5, the last retained digit remains the same.
- If the digit to be removed is 5 or greater, the last retained digit is increased by one.

# Chapter 3: Matter and Energy

## Looking at the Facts of Matter
### Solids
- Have a definite shape and volume.
- Particles are closely packed together and vibrate in place.

### Liquids
- Have a definite volume but take the shape of their container.
- Particles are close together but can move past one another.

### Gases
- Have neither a definite shape nor a definite volume.
- Particles are far apart and move freely.

## Ice in Alaska, Water in Texas: Changing States of Matter
### Boiling point
- The temperature at which a liquid turns into vapor.

### Freezing point
- The temperature at which a liquid turns into a solid.

### Sublimate this!
- Sublimation is the process where a solid turns directly into a gas without passing through the liquid state.

## Classifying Pure Substances and Mixtures
### Keeping it simple with pure substances
- A pure substance has a uniform and definite composition.

### Throwing mixtures into the mix
- Mixtures consist of two or more substances that are not chemically combined and can be separated by physical means.

## Nice Properties You’ve Got There
### Identifying substances by density
- Density is defined as mass per unit volume.

### How dense are you? Measuring density
- The formula for density is given by:
$$
\text{Density} = \frac{\text{Mass}}{\text{Volume}}
$$
```



```markdown
# Chapter 4: Something Smaller Than an Atom? Atomic Structure

## Taking an Up-Close Look at the Atom: Subatomic Particles
Atoms are composed of three main types of subatomic particles: protons, neutrons, and electrons. Protons are positively charged particles found in the nucleus of the atom, while neutrons have no charge and also reside in the nucleus. Electrons are negatively charged particles that orbit the nucleus in various energy levels.

## Taking Center Stage: The Nucleus
The nucleus is the central part of an atom and contains protons and neutrons. The number of protons in the nucleus determines the atomic number of an element, which defines the element itself. The mass of the nucleus is concentrated due to the presence of protons and neutrons, making it significantly heavier than the surrounding electron cloud.

## Locating the Electrons in an Atom
Electrons are located in regions around the nucleus called electron shells or energy levels. The arrangement of electrons in these shells is crucial for understanding chemical bonding and the behavior of atoms.

### The Bohr model — it’s really not boring
The Bohr model describes the atom as a small, positively charged nucleus surrounded by electrons that travel in circular orbits around the nucleus. Each orbit corresponds to a specific energy level, and electrons can move between these levels by absorbing or emitting energy.

### Quantum mechanical model
The quantum mechanical model of the atom provides a more accurate representation of electron behavior. Instead of fixed orbits, electrons exist in probabilistic clouds called orbitals, where the likelihood of finding an electron in a particular region is described by a mathematical function.

## Configuring Electrons (Bed Check for Electrons)

### Examining the energy-level diagram
An energy-level diagram visually represents the arrangement of electrons in an atom. Each energy level can hold a specific number of electrons, and the distribution of electrons across these levels determines the atom's chemical properties.

### Eyeing electron configurations
Electron configurations describe the distribution of electrons among the various energy levels and orbitals. The notation used for electron configurations indicates the number of electrons in each subshell, following the Aufbau principle, Hund's rule, and the Pauli exclusion principle.

### Living on the edge: Valence electrons
Valence electrons are the electrons in the outermost energy level of an atom. They play a critical role in chemical bonding and reactions, as they are the electrons that are involved in forming bonds with other atoms.

## Examining Isotopes and Ions

### Isolating the isotope
Isotopes are variants of a particular chemical element that have the same number of protons but different numbers of neutrons. This difference in neutron count results in different atomic masses for the isotopes of an element.

### Keeping an eye on ions
Ions are atoms or molecules that have gained or lost one or more electrons, resulting in a net electrical charge. Cations are positively charged ions formed by the loss of electrons, while anions are negatively charged ions formed by the gain of electrons.
```



# Repeating Patterns of Periodicity

## Understanding How Elements Are Arranged in the Periodic Table

### Classifying metals, nonmetals, and metalloids
- Metals are typically shiny, malleable, ductile, and good conductors of heat and electricity.
- Nonmetals are generally dull, brittle, and poor conductors.
- Metalloids have properties intermediate between metals and nonmetals.

### Organizing by periods and families
- The periodic table is organized into rows called periods and columns called groups or families.
- Elements in the same group often exhibit similar chemical properties.

# Chapter 6: Balloons, Tires, and Scuba Tanks: The Wonderful World of Gases

## Taking a Microscopic View of Gases: The Kinetic Molecular Theory
- The kinetic molecular theory explains the behavior of gases in terms of particles in motion.
- Gas particles are in constant, random motion and collide with each other and the walls of their container.

## Staying Under Pressure — Atmospheric Pressure, That Is

### Measuring atmospheric pressure: The barometer
- A barometer measures atmospheric pressure using a column of mercury or an aneroid mechanism.

### Measuring confined gas pressure: The manometer
- A manometer measures the pressure of a gas in a container relative to atmospheric pressure.

## Grasping Different Gas Laws

### Boyle’s law: Nothing to do with boiling
- Boyle's law states that the pressure of a gas is inversely proportional to its volume at constant temperature:
$$ P_1 V_1 = P_2 V_2 $$

### Charles’s law: Don’t call me Chuck
- Charles's law states that the volume of a gas is directly proportional to its temperature at constant pressure:
$$ \frac{V_1}{T_1} = \frac{V_2}{T_2} $$

### Gay-Lussac’s law
- Gay-Lussac's law states that the pressure of a gas is directly proportional to its temperature at constant volume:
$$ \frac{P_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2}{T_2} $$

### The combined gas law
- The combined gas law combines Boyle's, Charles's, and Gay-Lussac's laws:
$$ \frac{P_1 V_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2 V_2}{T_2} $$

### Avogadro’s law
- Avogadro's law states that equal volumes of gases at the same temperature and pressure contain an equal number of molecules:
$$ V \propto n $$

### The ideal gas equation
- The ideal gas equation relates pressure, volume, temperature, and the number of moles of a gas:
$$ PV = nRT $$

### The van der Waals equation
- The van der Waals equation accounts for the volume occupied by gas molecules and the attractive forces between them:
$$ \left(P + a \frac{n^2}{V^2}\right)(V - nb) = nRT $$

## Applying Gas Laws to Stoichiometry
- Gas laws can be applied to stoichiometric calculations involving reactions that produce or consume gases.

## Tackling Dalton’s and Graham’s Laws

### Dalton’s law
- Dalton's law of partial pressures states that the total pressure of a gas mixture is the sum of the partial pressures of each individual gas:
$$ P_{total} = P_1 + P_2 + P_3 + ... $$

### Graham’s law
- Graham's law states that the rate of effusion of a gas is inversely proportional to the square root of its molar mass:
$$ \frac{r_1}{r_2} = \sqrt{\frac{M_2}{M_1}} $$



# Chapter 7: Chemical Cooking: Chemical Reactions

## Knowing What You Have and What You’ll Get: Reactants and Products

### Understanding How Reactions Occur: The Collision Theory

The collision theory states that for a reaction to occur, reactant particles must collide with sufficient energy and proper orientation.

#### Eyeing a One-Step Collision Example
In a simple reaction, such as the combination of hydrogen and oxygen to form water, the molecules must collide effectively to break existing bonds and form new ones.

#### Considering an Exothermic Example
An exothermic reaction releases energy. For instance, the combustion of methane ($\text{CH}_4 + 2\text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{CO}_2 + 2\text{H}_2\text{O} + \text{energy}$) illustrates how energy is released when bonds are formed in the products.

#### Looking at an Endothermic Example
An endothermic reaction absorbs energy. A classic example is the decomposition of calcium carbonate ($\text{CaCO}_3 \rightarrow \text{CaO} + \text{CO}_2 + \text{energy}$), where energy is required to break the bonds in the reactants.

## Identifying Different Types of Reactions

1. **Combination Reactions**: Two or more reactants combine to form a single product.
- Example: $A + B \rightarrow AB$

2. **Decomposition Reactions**: A single compound breaks down into two or more products.
- Example: $AB \rightarrow A + B$

3. **Single-Displacement Reactions**: An element displaces another in a compound.
- Example: $A + BC \rightarrow AC + B$

4. **Double-Displacement Reactions**: The ions of two compounds exchange places in an aqueous solution to form two new compounds.
- Example: $AB + CD \rightarrow AD + CB$

5. **Combustion Reactions**: A substance combines with oxygen, releasing energy in the form of light or heat.
- Example: Hydrocarbon combustion.

6. **Redox Reactions**: Reactions involving the transfer of electrons between two species.
- Example: $Zn + Cu^{2+} \rightarrow Zn^{2+} + Cu$

## Balancing Chemical Reactions

Balancing chemical reactions is essential to ensure that the law of conservation of mass is upheld.

### Balancing Ammonia Production
The synthesis of ammonia can be represented as:
$$N_2 + 3H_2 \rightarrow 2NH_3$$
This equation is balanced with 1 nitrogen and 6 hydrogen atoms on both sides.

### Flicking the Lighter
The combustion of butane can be represented as:
$$2C_4H_{10} + 13O_2 \rightarrow 8CO_2 + 10H_2O$$
This equation is balanced, showing the conservation of atoms in the reaction.

# Chapter 8: The Mole: Can You Dig It?

## Counting Particles by Massing

The mole is a fundamental concept in chemistry that allows chemists to count particles by weighing them. One mole of any substance contains $6.022 \times 10^{23}$ entities (Avogadro's number).

## Using Moles to Count

Moles provide a bridge between the atomic scale and the macroscopic scale. The relationship between mass, moles, and molar mass is given by the equation:
$$ n = \frac{m}{M} $$
where:
- $n$ = number of moles
- $m$ = mass of the substance (in grams)
- $M$ = molar mass (in g/mol)



```markdown
# Understanding the Role of Moles in Chemical Reactions

## Making the calculations

### Determining what you need and what you’ll get: Reaction stoichiometry

Reaction stoichiometry involves using balanced chemical equations to calculate the amounts of reactants and products involved in a chemical reaction. The coefficients in a balanced equation represent the ratio of moles of each substance.

For example, in the reaction:

$$
aA + bB \rightarrow cC + dD
$$

The stoichiometric coefficients \(a\), \(b\), \(c\), and \(d\) indicate the number of moles of each reactant and product. If you know the number of moles of one substance, you can use the coefficients to find the moles of others.

### Figuring out the bang for your buck: Percent yield

Percent yield is a measure of the efficiency of a reaction, calculated using the formula:

$$
\text{Percent Yield} = \left( \frac{\text{Actual Yield}}{\text{Theoretical Yield}} \right) \times 100\%
$$

The actual yield is the amount of product obtained from the reaction, while the theoretical yield is the maximum amount predicted by stoichiometry.

### Running out of something and leaving something behind: Limiting reactants

In a chemical reaction, the limiting reactant is the substance that is completely consumed first, limiting the amount of product formed. To identify the limiting reactant, compare the mole ratio of the reactants used in the reaction to the mole ratio from the balanced equation.

# Mixing Matter Up: Solutions

## Getting Your Definitions Straight: Solutes, Solvents, and Solutions

A solution is a homogeneous mixture composed of two or more substances. The substance present in the greatest amount is called the solvent, while the substances present in lesser amounts are called solutes.

## Discussing solubility: How much solute will dissolve

Solubility is the maximum amount of solute that can dissolve in a given amount of solvent at a specific temperature and pressure. Factors affecting solubility include temperature, pressure, and the nature of the solute and solvent.

## Exploring saturation

A solution is saturated when it contains the maximum amount of solute that can dissolve at a given temperature. If more solute is added, it will not dissolve and will remain as a solid.

## Focusing on Solution Concentration Units

### Percent composition: Three different ratios

Percent composition can be expressed in three ways: mass percent, volume percent, and mole percent. Each ratio provides a different perspective on the concentration of a solute in a solution.

### Molarity: It’s number one!

Molarity (M) is defined as the number of moles of solute per liter of solution:

$$
M = \frac{n}{V}
$$

where \(n\) is the number of moles of solute and \(V\) is the volume of the solution in liters.

### Molality: Another use for the mole

Molality (m) is defined as the number of moles of solute per kilogram of solvent:

$$
m = \frac{n}{m_{solvent}}
$$

where \(m_{solvent}\) is the mass of the solvent in kilograms.

### Parts per million: The pollution unit

Parts per million (ppm) is a way of expressing very dilute concentrations of substances. It is defined as:

$$
\text{ppm} = \frac{\text{mass of solute}}{\text{mass of solution}} \times 10^6
$$

## Comprehending Colligative Properties of Solutions

### Reducing the pressure: Vapor-pressure lowering

Colligative properties depend on the number of solute particles in a solution, not their identity. One such property is vapor-pressure lowering, which states that the presence of a non-volatile solute decreases the vapor pressure of the solvent. This can be described by Raoult's Law:

$$
P_{solution} = X_{solvent} \cdot P^0_{solvent}
$$

where \(P_{solution}\) is the vapor pressure of the solution, \(X_{solvent}\) is the mole fraction of the solvent, and \(P^0_{solvent}\) is the vapor pressure of the pure solvent.
```



```markdown
# Chapter 10: Thermochemistry: Hot Stuff

## Looking at Reactions and Energy Changes

### Systems and surroundings
In thermochemistry, a system is the part of the universe we are focusing on, while the surroundings are everything else. The interaction between the system and its surroundings is crucial for understanding energy changes during chemical reactions.

### Heat
Heat is a form of energy that is transferred between systems or surroundings due to a temperature difference. It is important to measure heat changes during chemical reactions to understand the energy dynamics involved.

### Units of energy
The standard unit of energy in the International System of Units (SI) is the joule (J). Other units include calories (cal), where 1 cal = 4.184 J, and kilojoules (kJ), where 1 kJ = 1000 J.

### Heat capacities
Heat capacity is the amount of heat required to change the temperature of a substance by one degree Celsius (°C). It can be expressed as:
$$ C = \frac{q}{\Delta T} $$
where \( C \) is the heat capacity, \( q \) is the heat added, and \( \Delta T \) is the change in temperature.

### Calorimetry
Calorimetry is the measurement of heat changes during chemical reactions. A calorimeter is an instrument used to measure the heat absorbed or released during a reaction.

## Understanding Enthalpy Changes

### Finding Heats of Reaction
The heat of reaction, or enthalpy change (\( \Delta H \)), can be determined through various methods:

1. **Doing it yourself**: Conducting experiments to measure heat changes directly.
2. **Referring to tables**: Using tabulated values of enthalpies of formation to calculate \( \Delta H \).
3. **Relying on Hess’s law**: Applying Hess's law, which states that the total enthalpy change for a reaction is the sum of the enthalpy changes for individual steps, regardless of the pathway taken.

### Using standard heats of formation
The standard heat of formation (\( \Delta H_f^\circ \)) is the change in enthalpy when one mole of a compound is formed from its elements in their standard states. The heat of reaction can be calculated using:
$$ \Delta H = \sum \Delta H_f^\circ \text{(products)} - \sum \Delta H_f^\circ \text{(reactants)} $$

## Uncovering Enthalpies and Phase Transitions

# Chapter 11: Sour and Bitter: Acids and Bases

## Getting to Know the Properties of Acids and Bases: Macroscopic View
Acids and bases can be identified by their distinct properties. Acids typically taste sour, turn blue litmus paper red, and react with metals to produce hydrogen gas. Bases taste bitter, feel slippery, and turn red litmus paper blue.

## Recognizing Acids and Bases: Microscopic View
At the microscopic level, acids are substances that donate protons (H⁺ ions) in solution, while bases are substances that accept protons. This behavior can be described using the Brønsted-Lowry theory of acids and bases.

# Chapter 13: Opposites Do Attract: Ionic Bonding

## Magically Bonding Ions: Sodium + Chlorine = Table Salt

### Meeting the Components
- Sodium (Na) and Chlorine (Cl) react to form sodium chloride (NaCl).

### Understanding the Reaction
- The reaction can be represented as:
\[
\text{Na} (s) + \frac{1}{2} \text{Cl}_2 (g) \rightarrow \text{NaCl} (s)
\]

### Ending Up with a Bond
- The transfer of an electron from sodium to chlorine results in the formation of an ionic bond.

## Identifying Positive and Negative Ions: Cations and Anions
- Cations are positively charged ions, while anions are negatively charged ions.

## Grasping Polyatomic Ions
- Polyatomic ions consist of two or more atoms bonded together that carry a charge.

## Putting Ions Together: Ionic Compounds

### Putting Magnesium and Bromine Together
- Magnesium (Mg) and Bromine (Br) react to form magnesium bromide (MgBr₂).

### Applying the Crisscross Rule
- The crisscross rule is used to determine the formula of ionic compounds by balancing the charges of the ions.

## Naming Ionic Compounds
- Ionic compounds are named by combining the names of the cation and anion.

## Contrasting Electrolytes and Nonelectrolytes
- Electrolytes are substances that dissociate into ions in solution and conduct electricity, while nonelectrolytes do not.

# Chapter 14: Sharing Nicely: Covalent Bonding

## Eyeing Covalent Bond Basics
- Covalent bonds involve the sharing of electron pairs between atoms.

### Considering a Hydrogen Example
- The covalent bond in a hydrogen molecule (H₂) can be represented as:
\[
\text{H} + \text{H} \rightarrow \text{H}_2
\]

### Comparing Covalent Bonds with Other Bonds
- Covalent bonds differ from ionic bonds in that they involve shared electrons rather than the transfer of electrons.



```markdown
# Understanding Multiple Bonds

## Naming Binary Covalent Compounds

### Empirical Formula
The empirical formula represents the simplest whole-number ratio of elements in a compound.

### Molecular or True Formula
The molecular formula provides the actual number of atoms of each element in a molecule.

### Structural Formula
The structural formula shows the arrangement of atoms and the bonds between them.

## Sharing Electron Pairs — Sometimes Equally and Sometimes Not

### Attracting Electrons: Electronegativities
Electronegativity is a measure of the tendency of an atom to attract a bonding pair of electrons.

### Polar Covalent Bonding
In polar covalent bonds, electrons are shared unequally between atoms, leading to partial positive and negative charges.

### Wondering About Water: A Really Strange Molecule
Water (H₂O) is a polar molecule due to the bent shape and the difference in electronegativity between hydrogen and oxygen.

# Chapter 15: What Do Molecules Really Look Like?

## Molecular Geometry and Hybridization

### Seeing How Shape Matters
The shape of a molecule affects its physical and chemical properties.

### Getting Charged with Polarity
Molecules can be polar or nonpolar based on their shape and the electronegativities of the atoms involved.

### Predicting Polarity
The polarity of a molecule can be predicted by analyzing its shape and the distribution of electron density.

## Delving Into Electron and Molecular Geometry (VSEPR)
The Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) theory helps predict the geometry of molecules based on electron pair repulsion.

## Comprehending the Valence Bond Theory (Hybridization)
Valence bond theory explains how atomic orbitals combine to form hybrid orbitals, which are used in bonding.

## Breaking Down the Molecular Orbital (MO) Theory
Molecular orbital theory describes the behavior of electrons in molecules, where atomic orbitals combine to form molecular orbitals.

# Chapter 16: Tackling Periodic Trends

## Checking Out the Importance of Size
Atomic size and ionic size are important factors that influence the behavior of elements in the periodic table.

# Chapter 17: Examining the Link between Intermolecular Forces and Condensed States

## Understanding Types of Intermolecular Forces

- **Bringing ions and dipoles together**: Ionic interactions occur between charged ions and polar molecules, leading to significant attractions.

- **Mutually attracting dipoles**: Dipole-dipole interactions arise between polar molecules, where the positive end of one molecule attracts the negative end of another.

- **Drawing close to hydrogen**: Hydrogen bonding is a strong type of dipole-dipole interaction that occurs when hydrogen is bonded to highly electronegative atoms like nitrogen, oxygen, or fluorine.

- **Uniting through the cloud**: London dispersion forces, or van der Waals forces, are weak attractions that occur due to temporary dipoles in nonpolar molecules.

## Grasping the Properties of Liquids

- **Resisting an increase: Surface tension**: Surface tension is the energy required to increase the surface area of a liquid due to intermolecular forces at the surface.

- **Resisting to flow: Viscosity**: Viscosity is a measure of a liquid's resistance to flow, influenced by the strength of intermolecular forces and the size and shape of the molecules.

- **Climbing the walls: Capillary action**: Capillary action is the ability of a liquid to flow in narrow spaces without the assistance of external forces, driven by cohesive and adhesive forces.

- **Warming up: Heat capacity**: Heat capacity is the amount of heat energy required to raise the temperature of a substance, which is affected by the strength of intermolecular forces.

## Working with Solids

### Deciphering Phase Diagrams

Phase diagrams illustrate the states of matter (solid, liquid, gas) of a substance at various temperatures and pressures, showing the conditions under which each phase exists and transitions occur.



# Chapter 18: Cough! Cough! Hack! Hack! Air Pollution

## Seeing Where This Mess Began: Civilization’s Effect on the Atmosphere

### Taking a Closer Look at the Earth’s Atmosphere

- **The troposphere**: What humans affect the most
- **The stratosphere**: Protecting humans with the ozone layer

## Getting the Lowdown on the Ozone Layer

- **Explaining how the ozone reacts to gases**

The ozone layer is crucial for absorbing the majority of the sun's harmful ultraviolet radiation. Ozone ($O_3$) is formed when oxygen molecules ($O_2$) are dissociated by UV light into individual oxygen atoms, which then react with other $O_2$ molecules to form ozone.

- **Seeing how CFCs hurt the ozone layer**

Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs) are stable compounds that, when released into the atmosphere, eventually reach the stratosphere. There, UV radiation breaks them down, releasing chlorine atoms that catalyze the destruction of ozone:

\[
\text{Cl} + \text{O}_3 \rightarrow \text{ClO} + \text{O}_2
\]
\[
\text{ClO} + \text{O} \rightarrow \text{Cl} + \text{O}_2
\]

This cycle can continue, leading to significant depletion of the ozone layer.

## Comprehending the Greenhouse Effect

The greenhouse effect is a natural process where certain gases in the Earth's atmosphere trap heat, preventing it from escaping back into space. Key greenhouse gases include carbon dioxide ($CO_2$), methane ($CH_4$), and nitrous oxide ($N_2O$). The increase in these gases due to human activities enhances the greenhouse effect, leading to global warming.

## Breathing Brown Air: Photochemical Smog

- **London smog**: Historically, London experienced severe smog events due to coal burning, which released sulfur dioxide ($SO_2$) and particulate matter, leading to health crises.

- **Photochemical smog**: This type of smog occurs when sunlight reacts with pollutants such as nitrogen oxides ($NO_x$) and volatile organic compounds (VOCs), producing ozone at ground level, which is harmful to human health.

## “I’m Meltingggggg!” — Acid Rain

- **Don’t drink the water: What’s in acid rain**

Acid rain is formed when sulfur dioxide ($SO_2$) and nitrogen oxides ($NO_x$) are released into the atmosphere, where they react with water vapor to form sulfuric acid ($H_2SO_4$) and nitric acid ($HNO_3$):

\[
\text{SO}_2 + \text{H}_2O \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{SO}_4
\]
\[
\text{NO}_2 + \text{H}_2O \rightarrow \text{HNO}_3
\]

- **Charge them up and drop them out: Electrostatic precipitators**

Electrostatic precipitators are devices used to remove particles from exhaust gases. They use high-voltage electrical fields to charge particles, which are then collected on oppositely charged plates.

- **Washing water: Scrubbers**

Scrubbers are systems that remove pollutants from industrial exhaust streams. They typically use a liquid to absorb or neutralize harmful gases, such as sulfur dioxide, before they are released into the atmosphere.

# Beta Particle Emission
Beta particle emission involves the transformation of a neutron into a proton, emitting a beta particle (an electron) and an antineutrino. The reaction can be represented as:

$$
n \rightarrow p + e^- + \bar{\nu}
$$

# Gamma Radiation Emission
Gamma radiation emission occurs when an excited nucleus releases energy in the form of gamma rays. This process does not change the number of protons or neutrons in the nucleus.

# Positron Emission
Positron emission is a type of beta decay where a proton is converted into a neutron, emitting a positron (the antimatter counterpart of an electron) and a neutrino. The reaction is:

$$
p \rightarrow n + e^+ + \nu
$$

# Electron Capture
Electron capture is a process where an electron from the innermost energy level is captured by the nucleus, combining with a proton to form a neutron and emitting a neutrino:

$$
p + e^- \rightarrow n + \nu
$$

# Figuring Out When Radioactive Decay Happens: Half-Lives

## Determining Half-Lives
The half-life of a radioactive isotope is the time required for half of the radioactive atoms in a sample to decay. It can be calculated using the formula:

$$
N(t) = N_0 \left( \frac{1}{2} \right)^{\frac{t}{t_{1/2}}}
$$

where:
- $N(t)$ is the quantity remaining after time $t$,
- $N_0$ is the initial quantity,
- $t_{1/2}$ is the half-life.

## Safe Handling
Safe handling of radioactive materials involves using shielding, maintaining distance, and minimizing exposure time.

## Radioactive Dating
Radioactive dating uses the known half-lives of isotopes to determine the age of materials. For example, carbon-14 dating is used for organic materials.

# Initiating Reactions: Nuclear Fission

## Calculating Chain Reactions and Critical Mass
Nuclear fission occurs when a heavy nucleus splits into smaller nuclei, releasing energy. The critical mass is the minimum mass of fissile material needed to maintain a chain reaction.

## Controlling Reactions: Nuclear Power Plants
Nuclear power plants control fission reactions using control rods that absorb neutrons, regulating the rate of the reaction.

## Producing Plutonium with Breeder Reactors
Breeder reactors generate more fissile material than they consume, typically converting uranium-238 into plutonium-239.

# Harnessing Nuclear Fusion: The Hope for Tomorrow’s Energy

## Overcoming the Control Issues
Nuclear fusion, the process that powers the sun, involves combining light nuclei to form heavier nuclei, releasing energy. Controlling fusion reactions on Earth presents significant technical challenges.

## Imagining What the Future Holds
Future advancements in fusion technology could provide a nearly limitless source of energy with minimal environmental impact.

# Identifying the Effects of Radiation
Radiation can have various effects on living organisms, including cellular damage, increased cancer risk, and genetic mutations.

About the Author



John T. Moore, EdD, grew up in the foothills of western North
Carolina. He attended the University of North Carolina–
Asheville where he received his bachelor’s degree in chemistry.
He earned his master’s degree in chemistry from Furman
University in Greenville, South Carolina. After a stint in the
United States Army, he decided to try his hand at teaching. In
1971, he joined the chemistry faculty of Stephen F. Austin State
University in Nacogdoches, Texas, where he still teaches
chemistry. In 1985, he started back to school part-time, and in
1991 he received his doctorate in education from Texas A&M
University.

John’s area of specialty is chemical education. He has developed
several courses for students planning on teaching chemistry at
the high school level. In the early 1990s, he shifted his emphasis
to training elementary education majors and in-service
elementary teachers in hands-on chemical activities. He has
received four Eisenhower grants for professional development
of elementary teachers and has served as coeditor (along with
one of his former students) of the “Chemistry for Kids” feature
of The Journal of Chemical Education. He is the author of several
books on chemistry and is coauthor of several more, including
Biochemistry For Dummies and Organic Chemistry II For
Dummies.

Although teaching has always been foremost in his heart, John
found time to work part-time for almost five years in the medical
laboratory of the local hospital and has been a consultant for a
textbook publisher. He is active in a number of local, state, and
national organizations, such as the Nacogdoches Kiwanis Club
and the American Chemical Society.

John lives in the Piney Woods of East Texas with his wife Robin
and their two dogs and two cats. He enjoys brewing his own
beer and mead and making custom knife handles from exotic
woods. He also loves to cook. His two boys, Jason and Matt,



along with his daughter-in-law Sarah and two grandchildren,
Zane and Sadie, remain in the mountains of North Carolina.

# Introduction

You’ve passed the first hurdle in understanding a little about chemistry: You’ve picked up Chemistry For Dummies, 2nd Edition. I imagine that a large number of people looked at the title, saw the word chemistry, and bypassed it like it was covered in germs.

I don’t know how many times I’ve been on vacation, struck up a conversation with someone, and been asked the dreaded question: “What do you do?”

“I’m a teacher,” I reply.

“Really? And what do you teach?”

I steel myself, grit my teeth, and say in my most pleasant voice, “Chemistry.”

I see The Expression, followed by, “Oh, I never took chemistry. It was too hard.” Or “You must be smart to teach chemistry.” Or “Goodbye!” If I were still in the dating scene, “Hi, I teach chemistry” would not be a good pick-up line!

I think a lot of people feel this way because they think that chemistry is too abstract, too mathematical, too removed from their real lives. But in one way or another, all of us do chemistry.

Remember making that baking soda and vinegar volcano as a child? That’s chemistry. Do you cook or clean or use fingernail polish remover? All that is chemistry. I never had a chemistry set as a child, but I always loved science. My high school chemistry teacher was a great biology teacher but really didn’t know much chemistry. But when I took my first chemistry course in college, the labs hooked me. I enjoyed seeing the colors of the solids coming out of solutions. I enjoyed synthesis,
```



```markdown
Chemistry is sometimes called the central science because in order to have a good understanding of biology, geology, or even physics, you must have a good understanding of chemistry.

The goal of this book is to provide a basic understanding of some chemical topics that commonly appear in high school or college introductory chemistry courses.

Chemistry requires practice, similar to how one cannot become a tennis star just by watching. It is essential to work on problems, such as gas laws, and use textbooks for practice problems.

The process of learning chemistry can be enjoyable and rewarding.

feedback, I felt that I hadn’t included quite enough about
calculations and some other topics that students taking a
college or high school–level class really needed. So in this
second edition I beefed up the calculations and included some
extra topics normally found in the first year of high school
chemistry or the first semester of general chemistry in college.
Overall, this edition will be more useful to those of you taking
the chemistry course. For those of you who want some help
with second-semester topics, hang in there and maybe, just
maybe, you’ll soon see Chemistry II For Dummies in your local
bookstore.

Foolish Assumptions

I really don’t know why you bought this book (or will buy it —
in fact, if you’re still in the bookstore and haven’t bought it yet,
buy two and give one as a gift), but I assume that you’re taking
(or retaking) a chemistry course or preparing to take a
chemistry course. I also assume that you feel relatively
comfortable with arithmetic and know enough algebra to solve
for a single unknown in an equation. And I assume that you
have a scientific calculator capable of doing exponents and
logarithms.

And if you’re buying this book just for the thrill of finding out
about something different — with no plan of ever taking a
chemistry course — I applaud you and hope that you enjoy this
adventure. Feel free to skip those topics that don’t hold your
interest; for you, there will be no tests, only the thrill of
increasing your knowledge about something new.

What Not to Read



I know you’re a busy person and want to get just what you need
from this book. Although I want you to read every single word
I’ve written, I understand you may be on a time crunch. I keep
the material to the bare bones, but I include a few sidebars.
They’re interesting reading (again, at least to me) but not really
necessary for understanding the topic at hand, so feel free to
skip them. This is your book; use it any way you want.

I mark some paragraphs with Technical Stuff icons. What I tell
you in these paragraphs is more than you need to know, strictly
speaking, but it may give you helpful or interesting detail about
the topic at hand. If you want just the facts, you can skip these
paragraphs.

How This Book Is Organized

I present this book’s content in a logical progression of topics.
But this doesn’t mean you have to start at the beginning and
read to the end of the book. Each chapter is self-contained, so
feel free to skip around. Sometimes, though, you’ll get a better
understanding if you do a quick scan of a background section as
you’re reading. To help you find appropriate background
sections, I’ve placed “see Chapter X for more information”
cross-references here and there throughout the book.

Because I’m a firm believer in concrete examples, I also include
lots of illustrations and figures with the text. They really help in
the understanding of chemistry topics. And to help you with the
math, I break up problems into steps so that you can easily
follow exactly what I’m doing.

I’ve organized the topics in a logical progression — basically the
same way I organize my courses for science and non-science
majors. Following is an overview of each part of the book.

# Part I: The Basic Concepts of Chemistry

## Definition of Chemistry
Chemistry is the study of matter and the changes it undergoes. It is central to the sciences, connecting physics, biology, and environmental science.

## Chemical Calculations
### Factor Label Method
The factor label method, also known as dimensional analysis, is a technique used to convert units and solve problems in chemistry.

### SI (Metric) System
The International System of Units (SI) is the standard metric system used in scientific measurements. Key units include:
- Length: meter (m)
- Mass: kilogram (kg)
- Time: second (s)
- Temperature: Kelvin (K)

## States of Matter
There are three primary states of matter:
1. Solid
2. Liquid
3. Gas

### Phase Changes
Phase changes occur when matter transitions from one state to another, often accompanied by energy changes. Common phase changes include:
- Melting (solid to liquid)
- Freezing (liquid to solid)
- Vaporization (liquid to gas)
- Condensation (gas to liquid)
- Sublimation (solid to gas)
- Deposition (gas to solid)

## Atomic Structure
Atoms are composed of three main subatomic particles:
- Protons (positive charge, located in the nucleus)
- Neutrons (no charge, located in the nucleus)
- Electrons (negative charge, orbiting the nucleus)

## The Periodic Table
The periodic table organizes elements based on their atomic number and properties. It is an essential tool for chemists to understand element behavior and relationships.

## The Atomic Nucleus
The nucleus contains protons and neutrons, which are collectively known as nucleons. The number of protons determines the element's identity.

## Gases and Gas Laws
Gases exhibit unique behaviors described by several laws:
- **Boyle’s Law**: $P_1V_1 = P_2V_2$ (at constant temperature)
- **Charles’s Law**: $\frac{V_1}{T_1} = \frac{V_2}{T_2}$ (at constant pressure)
- **Gay-Lussac’s Law**: $\frac{P_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2}{T_2}$ (at constant volume)
- **Combined Gas Law**: $\frac{P_1V_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2V_2}{T_2}$
- **Ideal Gas Law**: $PV = nRT$ (where $n$ is the number of moles and $R$ is the ideal gas constant)
- **Avogadro’s Law**: $V \propto n$ (at constant temperature and pressure)

# Part II: A Cornucopia of Chemical Concepts

## Chemical Reactions
Chemical reactions involve the transformation of reactants into products. Different types of reactions include:
- Synthesis
- Decomposition
- Single replacement
- Double replacement
- Combustion
```



```markdown
## The Mole Concept

The mole is a fundamental unit in chemistry that allows for the quantification of reactants and products in chemical reactions. It provides a bridge between the atomic and macroscopic worlds, enabling chemists to calculate the amounts of substances involved in reactions.

### Calculating Concentrations

Solutions are homogeneous mixtures of two or more substances. The concentration of a solution is a measure of the amount of solute present in a given quantity of solvent or solution. Common ways to express concentration include molarity (M), which is moles of solute per liter of solution:

$$
\text{Molarity (M)} = \frac{\text{moles of solute}}{\text{liters of solution}}
$$

### Thermochemistry

Thermochemistry deals with the energy changes that occur during chemical reactions. Reactions can be classified as exothermic or endothermic:

- **Exothermic Reactions**: These reactions release energy, usually in the form of heat. The heat released can be calculated using the formula:

$$
q = m \cdot c \cdot \Delta T
$$

where:
- \( q \) = heat absorbed or released
- \( m \) = mass of the substance
- \( c \) = specific heat capacity
- \( \Delta T \) = change in temperature

- **Endothermic Reactions**: These reactions absorb energy from their surroundings. The calculation of heat absorbed follows the same formula.

### Acids and Bases

Acids are substances that donate protons (H⁺ ions) in a solution, while bases accept protons. The concentration of hydrogen ions in a solution can be expressed using pH:

$$
\text{pH} = -\log[H^+]
$$

where \([H^+]\) is the concentration of hydrogen ions in moles per liter.

## Quantum Theory and Bonding

Quantum theory describes the behavior of electrons in atoms, where they exhibit both particle-like and wave-like properties. This duality leads to the concept of probabilities in electron locations.

### Ionic Bonding

Ionic bonds form when electrons are transferred from one atom to another, resulting in the formation of charged ions. For example, table salt (NaCl) is formed from the ionic bonding of sodium (Na⁺) and chloride (Cl⁻) ions.

### Covalent Bonding

Covalent bonds occur when two atoms share electrons. Water (H₂O) is a classic example of covalent bonding, where each hydrogen atom shares an electron with the oxygen atom.

### Naming Ionic Compounds

Ionic compounds are named by combining the names of the cation (positive ion) and the anion (negative ion). For example, NaCl is named sodium chloride.

### Lewis Structures

Lewis structures are diagrams that represent the bonding between atoms in a molecule. They show the arrangement of electrons and help visualize the structure of covalent compounds.

### Periodic Trends and Intermolecular Forces

Periodic trends refer to the patterns observed in the properties of elements across the periodic table, such as electronegativity, atomic radius, and ionization energy. Intermolecular forces are the forces of attraction between molecules, which play a crucial role in determining the physical properties of substances.
```



```markdown
# Environmental Chemistry: Benefits and Problems

In this section, we discuss environmental issues, specifically air and water pollution. Understanding the chemistry behind these pollutants is essential for evaluating possible solutions to these pressing problems.

## Air Pollution

Air pollution is caused by various substances released into the atmosphere, including:

- **Carbon Monoxide (CO)**: Produced from incomplete combustion of fossil fuels.
- **Sulfur Dioxide (SO₂)**: Emitted from volcanic eruptions and industrial processes, particularly from burning coal.
- **Nitrogen Oxides (NOx)**: Result from vehicle emissions and industrial activities.
- **Particulate Matter (PM)**: Tiny particles that can penetrate the respiratory system, originating from various sources including construction sites and vehicle exhaust.

### Chemical Reactions Involved

1. **Formation of Acid Rain**:
\[
\text{SO}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{SO}_3
\]
\[
\text{H}_2\text{SO}_3 + \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{SO}_4
\]

2. **Ozone Formation**:
\[
\text{NO} + \text{O}_3 \rightarrow \text{NO}_2 + \text{O}_2
\]

## Water Pollution

Water pollution arises from various sources, including industrial discharge, agricultural runoff, and sewage. Key pollutants include:

- **Heavy Metals**: Such as lead (Pb) and mercury (Hg), which can accumulate in aquatic organisms.
- **Nutrients**: Excess nitrogen and phosphorus can lead to eutrophication, causing algal blooms.
- **Pathogens**: Bacteria and viruses that contaminate water sources.

### Chemical Reactions Involved

1. **Eutrophication**:
\[
\text{N}_2 + \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow \text{NH}_3 + \text{O}_2
\]

2. **Heavy Metal Contamination**:
\[
\text{Pb}^{2+} + 2\text{Cl}^- \rightarrow \text{PbCl}_2 \downarrow
\]

## Nuclear Chemistry

Nuclear chemistry involves the study of radioactivity, nuclear processes, and their applications. Key concepts include:

- **Radioactivity**: The process by which unstable atomic nuclei lose energy by emitting radiation.
- **Carbon-14 Dating**: A method used to determine the age of an object containing organic material by measuring the amount of carbon-14 it contains.

### Key Reactions

1. **Fission**:
\[
\text{n} + \text{U}^{235} \rightarrow \text{Ba}^{141} + \text{Kr}^{92} + 3\text{n} + \text{Energy}
\]

2. **Fusion**:
\[
\text{H}^{2} + \text{H}^{3} \rightarrow \text{He}^{4} + \text{n} + \text{Energy}
\]
```

- **Metric System**: Overview of the metric system used in scientific measurements.
- **Matter**: Discussion of matter and the states it can exist in.
- **Energy**: Explanation of different types of energy and how it is measured.

# Chapter 1

## What Is Chemistry, and Why Do I Need to Know Some?

Chemistry is the science that studies the composition, structure, properties, and changes of matter. It is a central science that connects physics with other natural sciences such as biology and geology. Understanding chemistry is essential for various fields, including medicine, engineering, environmental science, and many others.

### The Scientific Method

The scientific method is a systematic approach to research and experimentation. It typically involves the following steps:

1. **Observation**: Gathering data through the senses or instruments.
2. **Question**: Formulating a question based on observations.
3. **Hypothesis**: Proposing a testable explanation for the observations.
4. **Experimentation**: Conducting experiments to test the hypothesis.
5. **Analysis**: Analyzing the data collected during experiments.
6. **Conclusion**: Drawing conclusions based on the analysis and determining whether the hypothesis is supported or refuted.

### General Areas of Chemistry

Chemistry can be divided into several branches, including:

- **Organic Chemistry**: The study of carbon-containing compounds and their properties.
- **Inorganic Chemistry**: The study of inorganic compounds, typically those that do not contain carbon-hydrogen bonds.
- **Physical Chemistry**: The study of the physical properties and behavior of chemical systems.
- **Analytical Chemistry**: The study of the composition of materials and the development of tools used to measure properties.
- **Biochemistry**: The study of chemical processes within and related to living organisms.

### Expectations in a Chemistry Class

In a chemistry class, students can expect to engage in a variety of activities, including:

- Learning fundamental concepts and terminology.
- Conducting laboratory experiments to observe chemical reactions and properties.
- Solving problems using mathematical equations related to chemical principles.
- Collaborating with peers on projects and discussions.

Chemistry is not just about memorizing facts; it involves critical thinking, problem-solving, and a hands-on approach to learning about the world around us.
```



# Understanding What Chemistry Is

Chemistry is the study of matter, which is anything that has mass and occupies space. It focuses on the composition and properties of matter and the changes it undergoes, including energy changes.

Traditionally, science was divided into distinct areas: biology for living things, geology for rocks, chemistry for substances with odors, and physics for non-functional entities. However, modern science has blurred these lines, leading to fields such as biochemistry, chemical physics, and geochemistry. Despite this, chemistry remains centered on matter and energy and their transformations.

A significant aspect of chemistry involves the changes that matter undergoes. Matter can be classified as either pure substances or mixtures of pure substances. A chemical change, or chemical reaction, occurs when one substance transforms into another, resulting in the creation of a new substance.

## Compounds and Elements

Matter consists of pure substances or mixtures of pure substances, which are further categorized into elements and compounds.



# Distinguishing between Science and Technology

Science is far more than a collection of facts, figures, graphs, and tables. Science is a method for examining the physical universe. It’s a way of asking and answering questions. However, in order for it to be called science, it must be testable. Being testable is what makes science different from faith.

For example, you may believe in UFOs, but can you test for their existence? How about matters of love? Does she love me? How much does she love me? Can I design a test to test and quantify that love? I think not. I have to accept that love on faith. It’s not based in science, which is okay. Mankind has struggled with many great questions that science can’t answer. Science is a tool that is useful in examining certain questions, but not all. You wouldn’t use a front-end loader to eat a piece of pie, nor would you dig a ditch with a fork. Those are inappropriate tools for the task, just as science is an inappropriate tool for areas of faith.

Science is best described by the attitudes of scientists themselves: They’re skeptical. They simply won’t take another person’s word for a phenomena — it must be testable. And they hold onto the results of their experiments tentatively, waiting for another scientist to disprove them. Scientists wonder, they question, they strive to find out why, and they experiment — they have exactly the same attitudes that most small children have before they grow up. Maybe this is a good definition of scientists — they are adults who’ve never lost that wonder of nature and the desire to know.

Technology, the use of knowledge toward a very specific goal, actually developed before science. Ancient peoples cooked food, smelted ores, made beer and wine by fermentation, and...



```markdown
made drugs and dyes from plant material. Technology initially existed without much science. There were few theories and few true experiments. Reasoning was left to the philosophers. Eventually alchemy arose and gave chemistry its experimental basis. Alchemists searched for ways to turn other metals into gold and, in doing so, discovered many new chemical substances and processes, such as distillation. However, it wasn’t until the 17th century that experimentation replaced serendipity and true science began.

## Deciphering the Scientific Method

The scientific method is normally described as the way scientists go about examining the physical world around them. In fact, no one uses just one scientific method every time, but the one I cover here describes most of the critical steps scientists go through sooner or later.
```



# How the Scientific Method Works

The scientific method is a circular process that progresses from observations to hypotheses to experiments and back to observations. This iterative process may lead to the formulation of laws or theories.

## Steps of the Scientific Method

1. **Observations**: Scientists begin by making observations and noting facts about phenomena in the physical universe. These observations often raise questions or problems that the researcher aims to address.

2. **Hypothesis**: The researcher formulates a hypothesis, which is a tentative explanation consistent with the observations—essentially, an educated guess.

3. **Experimentation**: An experiment is designed to test the hypothesis. This experiment generates new observations or facts that can be used to refine the existing hypothesis or develop a new one.

4. **Iteration**: The process continues as more experiments are conducted, leading to further observations and modifications of hypotheses.

## Continuous Improvement

In good science, this loop of observations, hypotheses, and experimentation is perpetual. As scientists enhance their skills, develop better methods for examining nature, and create more advanced instruments, their hypotheses undergo repeated testing. Conclusions that seem scientifically valid today may be revised or disproven in the future.

Through this ongoing process, well-conducted experiments using the scientific method can lead researchers to propose scientific laws or theories.



# Scientific Laws and Theories

A law is a generalization of what happens in a scientific system. For example, the law of conservation of matter states that matter is neither created nor destroyed. However, scientific laws can be modified based on new facts. With the advent of the nuclear age, it was discovered that in nuclear reactions, a small amount of matter disappears and is converted to energy. Therefore, the law of conservation of matter was revised to state:

**In ordinary chemical reactions, matter is neither created nor destroyed.**

A theory or model is proposed to explain why something happens. It is similar to a hypothesis but is supported by much more evidence. The distinction between a theory and an opinion lies in the extensive experiments, observations, and data that support it—essentially, it is based on facts.

The predictive power of a theory or model is significant. If a scientist can use the model to understand the system well, they can make predictions based on it and verify these predictions through further experimentation. The results from these experiments can then be used to refine or modify the theory or model, creating a continuous loop in the scientific process. This process does not have a definitive end; as technology and methods for examining nature advance, scientists may find it necessary to adjust their theories or models.

## Science Fairs and the Scientific Method

Consider a high school student encouraged to participate in a local science fair. After contemplating various project ideas, they decide to investigate the energy content of nuts, inspired by a suggested experiment.



```markdown
## How You Can Use the Scientific Method

To test your hypothesis about the energy content in different types of peanuts, you can design an experiment using a calorimeter. A calorimeter can be constructed from steel cans and a thermometer. When designing your experiment, consider the following variables:

- Mass of water
- Mass of the nuts

Once your apparatus is built, you will need to conduct several trials for each type of peanut to ensure accurate results. Collect your data methodically and perform an error analysis to assess the reliability of your findings.

After analyzing your data, you may need to modify your initial hypothesis based on the results. This process may lead you to further questions, such as whether cashews contain more energy per gram than peanuts or how other nuts compare in energy content. This iterative nature of scientific inquiry is fundamental to the scientific method, where each investigation can lead to new questions and discoveries.

In addition to the scientific method, many significant discoveries in science occur through serendipity—accidental discoveries that happen unexpectedly. Recognizing the potential of these accidental findings requires a disciplined and trained scientific mindset.
```



Most people use the scientific method in their everyday lives without even thinking about it. You just think of it as tackling a problem logically. For example, suppose you buy that new HD TV and home theater system you’ve been wanting. You even buy a new CD changer so that you can listen to hours of music while studying. After unpacking and hooking everything up, you notice that you have no sound coming out of the left speakers when a CD is playing. You’ve identified a problem to investigate. Now you need to apply the scientific method to solve the problem. Here are some general steps to use:

1. **Develop a hypothesis about what you’re studying.**

This hypothesis is an educated guess you make about what you think the end results will be. A hypothesis gives you an idea of what to expect, although after you conduct your experiments, you may determine the hypothesis is invalid. For example, in the case of the dead left speakers, you may think that the problem lies with the CD changer, the receiver, or the cables connecting the two because everything else is working correctly. You form the hypothesis that something is wrong with the CD cables, that perhaps the left wire is broken or its connection is bad. You decide to experiment.

### Looking at the Branches of Chemistry

The general field of chemistry is so huge that it was originally subdivided into a number of different areas of specialization. But the different areas of chemistry now have a tremendous amount of overlap, just as there is among the various sciences. Here are the traditional fields of chemistry:

- **Analytical chemistry**: This branch is highly involved in the analysis of substances. Chemists from this field of chemistry may be trying to find out what substances are in a mixture (qualitative analysis) or how much of a substance is present (quantitative analysis).
```



```markdown
## Analytical Chemistry
Analytical chemists typically work in industry in product development or quality control. If a chemical manufacturing process goes wrong and is costing that industry hundreds of thousands of dollars an hour, that quality control chemist is under a lot of pressure to fix it and fix it fast. A lot of instrumentation is used in analytical chemistry.

## Biochemistry
This branch specializes in living organisms and systems. Biochemists study the chemical reactions that occur at the molecular level of an organism — the level where items are so small that people can’t directly see them. Biochemists study processes such as digestion, metabolism, reproduction, respiration, and so on. Sometimes, distinguishing between a biochemist and a molecular biologist is difficult because they both study living systems at a microscopic level. However, a biochemist really concentrates more on the reactions that are occurring.

## Biotechnology
This relatively new area of science is commonly placed with chemistry. It’s the application of biochemistry and biology when creating or modifying genetic material or organisms for specific purposes. It’s used in such areas as cloning and the creation of disease-resistant crops, and it has the potential for eliminating genetic diseases in the future.

## Inorganic Chemistry
This branch is involved in the study of inorganic compounds such as salts. It includes the study of the structure and properties of these compounds. It also commonly involves the study of the individual elements of the compounds.
```



```markdown
Chemists would probably say that it is the study of everything except carbon, which they leave to the organic chemists.

### Organic Chemistry
This is the study of carbon and its compounds. It’s probably the most organized of the areas of chemistry — with good reason. There are millions of organic compounds, with thousands more discovered or created each year. Industries such as the polymer industry, the petrochemical industry, and the pharmaceutical industry depend on organic chemists.

### Physical Chemistry
This branch figures out how and why a chemical system behaves as it does. Physical chemists study the physical properties and behavior of matter and try to develop models and theories that describe this behavior.

Chemists, no matter what the type, all tend to examine the world around them in two ways — a macroscopic view and a microscopic view.

### Macroscopic versus Microscopic Viewpoints
Most chemists that I know operate quite comfortably in two worlds. One is the macroscopic world that you and I see, feel, and touch. It’s the world of stained lab coats — of weighing out things like sodium chloride to create things like hydrogen gas. The macroscopic realm is the world of experiments, or what some nonscientists call the “real world.”
```



```markdown
But chemists also operate quite comfortably in the microscopic world that you and I can’t directly see, feel, or touch. Here, chemists work with theories and models. They may measure the volume and pressure of a gas in the macroscopic world, but they have to mentally translate the measurements into how close the gas particles are in the microscopic world.

Scientists often become so accustomed to slipping back and forth between these two worlds that they do so without even realizing it. An occurrence or observation in the macroscopic world generates an idea related to the microscopic world, and vice versa. You may find this flow of ideas disconcerting at first. But as you study chemistry, you’ll soon adjust so that it becomes second nature.

## Pure versus applied chemistry

In pure chemistry, chemists are free to carry out whatever research interests them — or whatever research they can get funded. They don’t necessarily expect to find a practical application for their research at this point. The researchers simply want to know for the sake of knowledge. This type of research (often called basic research) is most commonly conducted at colleges and universities. Chemists use undergraduate and graduate students to help conduct the research. The work becomes part of the professional training of the student. The researchers publish their results in professional journals for other chemists to examine and attempt to refute. Funding is almost always a problem, because the experimentation, chemicals, and equipment are quite expensive.

In applied chemistry, chemists normally work for private corporations. Their research is directed toward a very specific short-term goal set by the company — product improvement or the development of a disease-resistant strain of corn.
```



```markdown
example. Normally, more money is available for equipment and instrumentation with applied chemistry, but the chemists also have the pressure of meeting the company’s goals.

These two types of chemistry, pure and applied, share the same basic differences as science and technology. In science, the goal is simply the basic acquisition of knowledge without any need for apparent practical application. Science is simply knowledge for knowledge’s sake. Technology is the application of science toward a very specific goal.

Our society has a place for science and technology — likewise for the two types of chemistry. The pure chemist generates data and information that is then used by the applied chemist. Both types of chemists have their own sets of strengths, problems, and pressures. In fact, because of the dwindling federal research dollars, many universities are becoming much more involved in gaining patents, and they’re being paid for technology transfers into the private sector.

Chemists also work on improving production processes for established products.

In theoretical chemistry, chemists create models and test the predictive power of theories using computers, often without the need for a physical laboratory. They measure the physical properties of substances, including melting points, boiling points, and the strength of materials like polymers.

## Quality Control Chemist
These chemists analyze raw materials, intermediate products, and final products for purity to make sure that they fall within specifications. They may also offer technical support for the customer or analyze returned products. Many of these chemists often solve problems when they occur within the manufacturing process.

## Industrial Research Chemist
Chemists in this profession perform a large number of physical and chemical tests on materials. They may develop new products or work on improving existing products, possibly working with particular customers to formulate products that meet specific needs. They may also supply technical support to customers.

## Sales Representative
Chemists may work as sales representatives for companies that sell chemicals or pharmaceuticals. They may call on their customers and let them know of new products being developed, or they may help their customers solve problems.

## Forensic Chemist
These chemists analyze samples taken from crime scenes or analyze samples for the presence of drugs. They may also be called to testify in court as expert witnesses.

## Environmental Chemist
These chemists may work for water purification plants, the Environmental Protection Agency, the Department of Energy, or similar agencies. This type of work appeals to people who like chemistry but also like to get out in nature. They often go out to sites to collect their own samples.

## Preservationist of Art and Historical Works
Chemists work to restore paintings or statues, and sometimes they work to detect forgeries. With air and water pollution destroying works of art daily, these chemists preserve our heritage.

## Chemical Educator
Chemists working as educators may teach physical science and chemistry in schools. University chemistry teachers often conduct research and work with graduate students.
