```markdown
Chemists can’t imagine doing much of anything without having access to the periodic table. Instead of mastering the properties of 118+ elements (more are created almost every year), chemists — and chemistry students — can simply get a firm grasp of the properties of families of elements, thus saving a lot of time and effort.
```



```markdown
## Understanding How Elements Are Arranged in the Periodic Table

The elements are arranged in order of increasing atomic number. The atomic number (number of protons) is located right above the element symbol. Under the element symbol is the atomic mass, or atomic weight (sum of the protons and neutrons). Atomic mass is a weighted average of all naturally occurring isotopes.

Notice that two rows of elements — Ce–Lu (commonly called the lanthanides) and Th–Lr (the actinides) — have been pulled out of the main body of the periodic table. If they were included in the main body of the periodic table, the table would be much wider.

Using the periodic table, you can classify the elements in many ways.
```



# Metals, Nonmetals, and Metalloids

## Classifying Metals, Nonmetals, and Metalloids

Most of the elements in the periodic table are considered metals. The metals have properties that you normally associate with the metals you encounter in everyday life. They are solid (with the exception of mercury, $Hg$, a liquid), shiny, good conductors of electricity and heat, ductile (they can be drawn into thin wires), and malleable (they can be easily hammered into very thin sheets). All these metals tend to lose electrons easily.



All the elements on the left-hand side and in the middle of the
periodic table are metals, with one notable exception —



Hydrogen is the first element and is unique in its properties. Scientists place it above lithium, but it does not react like a metal. The elements to the right of the periodic table, except for a few that border the metals on the right, are classified as nonmetals (along with hydrogen).



Nonmetals have properties opposite those of the metals. The nonmetals are brittle, not malleable or ductile, poor conductors of both heat and electricity, and tend to gain electrons in chemical reactions. Some nonmetals are liquids; some are gases.

The elements that border the metals and the nonmetals are classified as metalloids.



The metalloids, or semimetals, have properties that are somewhat of a cross between metals and nonmetals. They tend to be economically important because of their unique conductivity properties (they only partially conduct).



```markdown
The periodic table is composed of horizontal rows called periods. The periods are numbered 1 through 7 on the left-hand side of the table. The atomic numbers increase from left to right in each period.

Even though they’re in the same period, these elements have chemical properties that are not all that similar. Consider the first two members of period 3: sodium (Na) and magnesium (Mg). In reactions, they both tend to lose electrons (after all, they are metals), but sodium loses one electron, whereas magnesium loses two. Chlorine (Cl), down near the end of the period, tends to gain an electron (it’s a nonmetal).

The vertical columns are called groups, or families. The families may be labeled at the top of the columns in one of two ways. The older method uses Roman numerals and letters. Many chemists prefer and still use this method. The newer method simply uses the numbers 1 through 18. The older method helps relate the position of an element on the periodic table with its number of valence electrons more than the 1–18 grouping system does.

The members of a family do have similar properties. Consider the IA family, starting with lithium (Li).
```



```markdown
Hydrogen, because it’s unique and doesn’t really fit anywhere — and going through francium (Fr). All these elements tend to lose only one electron in reactions. And all the members of the VIIA family tend to gain one electron.

So why do the elements in the same family have similar properties? And why do some families have the particular properties of electron loss or gain? To find out, you can examine four specific families on the periodic table and look at the electron configurations for a few elements in each family.

### My family name is special

The IA family is made up of the alkali metals. In reactions, these elements all tend to lose a single electron. This family contains some important elements, such as sodium (Na) and potassium (K). Both of these elements play an important role in the chemistry of the body and are commonly found in salts.

The IIA family is made up of the alkaline earth metals. All these elements tend to lose two electrons. Calcium (Ca) is an important member of the IIA family (you need calcium for healthy teeth and bones).

The VIIA family is made up of the halogens. They all tend to gain a single electron in reactions. Important members in the family include chlorine (Cl), used in making table salt and bleach, and iodine (I). Tincture of iodine is sometimes used as a disinfectant.

The VIIIA family is made up of the noble gases. These elements are very unreactive. For a long time, the noble gases were called the inert gases, because people...
```

```markdown
thought that these elements wouldn’t react at all. Later, a scientist named Neil Bartlett showed that at least some of the inert gases could be reacted, but they required very special conditions. After Bartlett’s discovery, the gases were then referred to as noble gases.

What valence electrons have to do with families

Chapter 4 explains that an electron configuration shows the number of electrons in each orbital in a particular atom. The electron configuration forms the basis of the concept of
```

```markdown
| Element | Electron Configuration |
|---------|-----------------------|
| Li      | $1s^2 2s^1$           |
| Na      | $1s^2 2s^2 2p^6 3s^1$ |
```

# Element Electron Configuration

## Electron Configurations

- Ne: $1s^2 2s^2 2p^6$
- Ar: $1s^2 2s^2 2p^6 3s^2 3p^6$
- Kr: $1s^2 2s^2 2p^6 3s^2 3p^6 4s^2 3d^{10} 4p^6$

These electron configurations show that each group of elements has some similarities in terms of the elements’ valence electrons. Valence electrons are the s and p electrons in the outermost energy level of an atom.

### Alkali Metals

In lithium, energy level 1 is filled, and a single electron is in the 2s orbital. In sodium, energy levels 1 and 2 are filled, and a single electron is in energy level 3. All these elements have one valence electron in an s orbital.

### Alkaline Earth Elements

The alkaline earth elements each have two valence electrons.

### Halogens

The halogens each have seven valence electrons (in s and p orbitals — d orbitals don’t count).

### Noble Gases

The noble gases each have eight valence electrons, which fill their valence orbitals.

## Valence Electrons and Roman Numerals

For the families labeled with a Roman numeral and an A, the Roman numeral gives the number of valence electrons.

- The IA family has 1 valence electron.
- The IIA family has 2 valence electrons.
- The VIIA family has 7 valence electrons.
- The VIIIA family has 8 valence electrons.

For example, oxygen (O) has six valence electrons (it’s in the VIA family), silicon (Si) has four, and so on. You don’t even have to write the electronic configuration or the energy diagram to determine the number of valence electrons.



# Noble and Gassy

The noble gases have eight valence electrons, filling their valence (outermost) energy level, which explains their extreme reluctance to react. A filled valence level makes an element stable, as it does not easily lose, gain, or share electrons.

A lot of stability in nature seems to be associated with this condition. Chemists observe that the other elements in the A families on the periodic table tend to lose, gain, or share valence electrons to achieve a filled valence shell of eight electrons. This phenomenon is sometimes called the octet rule.

For example, consider the electron configuration for sodium (Na):

$$
1s^2 2s^2 2p^6 3s^1
$$

Sodium has one valence electron — the $3s^1$. If it loses that electron, its valence shell would be at energy level 2, which is filled. Without the $3s^1$, it would become isoelectronic with neon (Ne), meaning the two elements have the same electronic configuration, thus achieving stability. Achieving stability by having a filled valence shell is the driving force in chemical bonding.

Elements labeled with a Roman numeral and a B, found in the middle of the periodic table, are commonly called transition metals. Their electrons progressively fill the d orbitals. Scandium (Sc) is the first member of the transition metals, with an electronic configuration of

$$
1s^2 2s^2 2p^6 3s^2 3p^6 4s^2 3d^1
$$

Titanium (Ti), the next transition metal, has a configuration of

$$
1s^2 2s^2 2p^6 3s^2 3p^6 4s^2 3d^2
$$

Notice that the number of electrons in the s and p orbitals doesn’t change, but the progressively added electrons fill the d orbitals. Lanthanides and actinides, the two groups of elements pulled out of the main body of the periodic table and shown below it, are classified as inner transition metals. In these elements, the electrons progressively fill the f orbitals.



```
the same way that the electrons of the transition metals fill the d
orbitals.
```



# Chapter 6: Balloons, Tires, and Scuba Tanks: The Wonderful World of Gases

Gases are all around you. Because gases are generally invisible, you may not think of them directly, but you’re certainly aware of their properties. You breathe a mixture of gases that you call air. You check the pressure of your automobile tires, and you check the atmospheric pressure to see if a storm is coming. You burn gases in your gas grill and lighters. You fill birthday balloons with gases for your loved ones.

The properties of gases and their interrelationships are important to you. Is there enough pressure in my tires? How big is that balloon going to be? Do my scuba tanks have enough air? The list goes on and on.

In this chapter, I introduce you to gases at both the microscopic and macroscopic levels. I show you one of science’s most successful theories — the kinetic molecular theory of gases. I explain the macroscopic properties of gases and show you the important interrelationships among them. I also show you how these relationships come into play in reaction stoichiometry.

## Kinetic Molecular Theory of Gases

The kinetic molecular theory (KMT) provides a model for understanding the behavior of gases. The main postulates of KMT are:

1. Gases consist of a large number of particles that are in constant random motion.
2. The volume of the gas particles is negligible compared to the volume of the container.
3. The particles collide with each other and with the walls of the container, and these collisions are perfectly elastic.
4. There are no attractive or repulsive forces between the particles.
5. The average kinetic energy of the gas particles is directly proportional to the temperature of the gas in Kelvin.

The relationship between temperature and kinetic energy can be expressed mathematically as:

\[
KE = \frac{3}{2} k T
\]

where \( KE \) is the average kinetic energy, \( k \) is the Boltzmann constant, and \( T \) is the temperature in Kelvin.

## Gas Laws

### Boyle's Law

Boyle's Law states that the pressure of a gas is inversely proportional to its volume when the temperature is held constant. Mathematically, this can be expressed as:

\[
P_1 V_1 = P_2 V_2
\]

where \( P \) is the pressure and \( V \) is the volume.

### Charles's Law

Charles's Law states that the volume of a gas is directly proportional to its temperature when the pressure is held constant. This can be expressed as:

\[
\frac{V_1}{T_1} = \frac{V_2}{T_2}
\]

where \( T \) is the temperature in Kelvin.

### Avogadro's Law

Avogadro's Law states that the volume of a gas is directly proportional to the number of moles of gas when the temperature and pressure are held constant. This can be expressed as:

\[
\frac{V_1}{n_1} = \frac{V_2}{n_2}
\]

where \( n \) is the number of moles of gas.

### Ideal Gas Law

The ideal gas law combines the previous laws into a single equation:

\[
PV = nRT
\]

where \( R \) is the ideal gas constant.

## Applications of Gas Laws

Understanding gas laws is crucial for various applications, including calculating the behavior of gases in different conditions, such as in tires, balloons, and scuba tanks. For example, when the temperature of a gas increases, its volume will also increase if the pressure remains constant, which is essential for understanding how balloons expand when heated.

In reaction stoichiometry, the ideal gas law can be used to relate the volume of gases involved in chemical reactions at standard temperature and pressure (STP).



# Taking a Microscopic View of Gases: The Kinetic Molecular Theory

A theory is useful to scientists if it describes the physical system they’re examining and allows them to predict what will happen if they change some variable. The kinetic molecular theory of gases does just that. It has limitations — all theories do — but it’s one of the most useful theories in chemistry. This section describes the theory’s basic postulates — assumptions, hypotheses, axioms (pick your favorite word) you can accept as being true.

1. Gases are composed of tiny particles, either atoms or molecules.
2. Unless you’re discussing matter at greatly elevated temperatures, the particles referred to as gases tend to be relatively small. The more massive particles clump together to form liquids or even solids. So gases are normally small with relatively low atomic and molecular weights.
3. The gas particles are so small when compared to the distances between them that the volume the gas particles themselves take up is negligible and is assumed to be zero.
4. These gas particles do take up some volume — that’s one of the properties of matter. But the gas particles are small, so if a container doesn’t hold many of them, you say that their volume is negligible when compared to the volume of the container or the space between the gas particles. Because of all that space between the gas particles...



```markdown
Particles can be squeezed together to compress a gas. Solids and liquids cannot be squeezed because their particles are much closer together.

The concept of a negligible quantity is significant in chemistry. For example, in the context of the acid ionization constant ($K_a$) of a weak acid, the amount of weak acid that has ionized can be ignored compared to the initial concentration of the acid.

In practical terms, this concept can be likened to finding a dollar in the street. For someone with no money, that dollar represents a significant quantity of cash. However, for a multimillionaire, that dollar is negligible compared to their overall wealth. Similarly, while gas particles have a volume, it is so small that it is insignificant compared to the distance between the gas particles and the volume of the container.

Gas particles are in constant random motion, moving in straight lines and colliding with the walls of the container. They possess higher kinetic energy than solids or liquids, which contributes to their motion. The particles continue to move in straight lines until they collide with each other or with the walls of the container, and they all move in different directions.
```



The collision of gas particles with the inside walls of a container is referred to as pressure. Gas particles are in constant, random, straight-line motion, which explains the uniform mixing of gases in a container. For example, when a bottle of cheap perfume is dropped at one end of a room, the scent reaches the other end quickly due to this motion.

Gas particles are assumed to have negligible attractive or repulsive forces between them, meaning they are considered independent. This assumption, while not entirely true, is useful because the forces are generally small enough to be ignored, especially for nonpolar gases like hydrogen and nitrogen, where the attractive forces are London forces. However, for polar gases such as water and HCl, this assumption may lead to inaccuracies.

Collisions between gas particles are assumed to be elastic, meaning that the total kinetic energy before and after the collision remains constant.



The amount of kinetic energy of the two gas particles remains the same. Gas particles collide with the walls of the container and with each other. When they collide, no kinetic energy is lost, but it may be transferred from one particle to another. For example, if a fast-moving particle collides with a slow-moving particle, kinetic energy is transferred from the faster particle to the slower one. The slow-moving particle gains energy and moves away at a greater speed, while the faster particle loses energy and moves away at a slower speed. The total amount of kinetic energy remains constant, illustrating the principle of energy transfer.

The Kelvin temperature is directly proportional to the average kinetic energy of the gas particles. Not all gas particles have the same kinetic energy; some move slowly while others move quickly, with most having speeds in between. Temperature, particularly measured on the Kelvin scale, is related to the average kinetic energy of the gas. Heating the gas increases the Kelvin temperature (K), which in turn increases the average kinetic energy. The relationship for converting Celsius to Kelvin is given by:

\[
K = °C + 273
\]



A gas that obeys all the postulates of the kinetic molecular theory is called an ideal gas. No real gas obeys the assumptions made in the second and fourth postulates exactly; all gas particles actually do have small measures of volume and attractive or repulsive force. However, a nonpolar gas at high temperatures and low pressure approaches ideal gas behavior.

### Atmospheric Pressure

The gas molecules of the atmosphere are constantly exerting a force called atmospheric pressure. Atmospheric pressure is measured using an instrument called a barometer.

### Measuring Atmospheric Pressure: The Barometer

Atmospheric pressure is typically included in weather reports, and changes in atmospheric pressure can indicate changes in the weather. The atmospheric pressure is measured using a barometer.



```
## Pressure

Atmospheric pressure at sea level is 760 mm of mercury (Hg).

The pressure due to the weight of a column of mercury can be calculated using the formula:

$$ P = h \cdot \rho \cdot g $$

where:
- \( P \) is the pressure,
- \( h \) is the height of the mercury column (in meters),
- \( \rho \) is the density of mercury (approximately \( 13,600 \, \text{kg/m}^3 \)),
- \( g \) is the acceleration due to gravity (approximately \( 9.81 \, \text{m/s}^2 \)).

At sea level, the height \( h \) of the mercury column is 0.760 m, leading to:

$$ P = 0.760 \, \text{m} \cdot 13,600 \, \text{kg/m}^3 \cdot 9.81 \, \text{m/s}^2 $$

Calculating this gives:

$$ P \approx 101,325 \, \text{Pa} $$

This value represents the standard atmospheric pressure at sea level.
```



A barometer is composed of a long glass tube that’s closed at one end and totally filled with a liquid. Barometers typically use mercury because it’s a very dense liquid. The tube filled with mercury is inverted into an open container of mercury so that the open end of the tube is under the surface of the mercury in the container. The force of gravity pulls the mercury in the tube down, causing it to drain out into the container, while the weight of the gases in the atmosphere exerts pressure downward on the mercury in the open container and forces it up into the tube. These forces balance, and the mercury in the tube comes to rest at a certain height. The greater the pressure of the atmosphere, the higher the mercury column that can be measured; the lower the pressure of the atmosphere, the shorter the column. At sea level, the column is 760 millimeters high, the so-called normal atmospheric pressure.

Atmospheric pressure can be expressed in various units:

- Millimeters of mercury (mm Hg)
- Atmospheres (atm), where 1 atmosphere is the pressure at sea level
- Torr, where 1 torr equals 1 millimeter of mercury
- Pounds per square inch (psi)
- Pascals (Pa), where 1 pascal equals 1 newton per square meter
- Kilopascals (kPa), where 1 kilopascal equals 1,000 pascals

The atmospheric pressure at sea level can be expressed as:

$$
760 \text{ mm Hg} = 1 \text{ atm} = 760 \text{ torr} = 14.69 \text{ psi} = 101,325 \text{ Pa} = 101.325 \text{ kPa}
$$

Additionally, atmospheric pressure is sometimes reported as inches of mercury:

$$
1 \text{ atm} = 29.921 \text{ in Hg}
$$



```markdown
## Measuring Confined Gas Pressure: The Manometer

You can measure the pressure of a gas confined in a container by using an apparatus called a manometer.
```



A manometer is a device used to measure gas pressure. It consists of a U-shaped glass tube partially filled with mercury, with one end sealed. The pressure of the gas in the container balances the mercury column, and the difference in mercury levels indicates the gas pressure.

### Gas Laws

Various scientific laws describe the relationships among four important physical properties of gases:

- Volume
- Pressure
- Temperature
- Amount

#### Boyle’s Law

Boyle’s Law states that the pressure of a gas is inversely proportional to its volume when the temperature and amount of gas are held constant. Mathematically, this can be expressed as:

$$ P_1 V_1 = P_2 V_2 $$

where \( P \) is pressure and \( V \) is volume.

#### Charles’s Law

Charles’s Law states that the volume of a gas is directly proportional to its temperature (in Kelvin) when the pressure and amount of gas are held constant. This relationship is expressed as:

$$ \frac{V_1}{T_1} = \frac{V_2}{T_2} $$

where \( T \) is temperature.

#### Gay-Lussac’s Law

Gay-Lussac’s Law states that the pressure of a gas is directly proportional to its temperature (in Kelvin) when the volume and amount of gas are held constant. This can be expressed as:

$$ \frac{P_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2}{T_2} $$

#### Avogadro’s Law

Avogadro’s Law states that equal volumes of gases, at the same temperature and pressure, contain an equal number of molecules. This law allows for variations in the amount of gas while keeping other properties constant.

#### Ideal Gas Law

The Ideal Gas Law combines all the individual gas laws into one equation that relates pressure, volume, temperature, and amount of gas:

$$ PV = nRT $$

where:
- \( P \) = pressure
- \( V \) = volume
- \( n \) = number of moles of gas
- \( R \) = ideal gas constant
- \( T \) = temperature in Kelvin



## Boyle’s Law

Boyle’s law, named after Robert Boyle, a 17th-century English scientist, describes the pressure-volume relationship of gases if the temperature and amount are kept constant.

The relationship can be expressed mathematically as:

$$ P_1 V_1 = P_2 V_2 $$

where:
- \( P_1 \) and \( V_1 \) are the initial pressure and volume,
- \( P_2 \) and \( V_2 \) are the final pressure and volume.

When the volume of a gas is decreased, the same number of gas particles is now contained in a much smaller volume, leading to an increase in the number of collisions between gas particles and the walls of the container. Consequently, the pressure increases.



Boyle’s law states that an inverse relationship exists between the volume and the pressure. As the volume decreases, the pressure increases, and vice versa. Boyle determined that the product of the pressure and the volume is a constant ($k$):

$$PV = k$$



Now consider a case where you have a gas at a certain pressure ($P_1$) and volume ($V_1$). If you change the volume to some new value ($V_2$), the pressure also changes to a new value ($P_2$). You can use Boyle’s law to describe both sets of conditions:

\[
P_1 V_1 = k
\]
\[
P_2 V_2 = k
\]

The constant, $k$, is the same in both cases. So you can say

\[
P_1 V_1 = P_2 V_2 \quad (\text{with temperature and amount constant})
\]

This equation is another statement of Boyle’s law — and it’s really a more useful one, because you’ll normally deal with changes in pressure and volume. If you know three of the preceding quantities, you can calculate the fourth one. For example:

Suppose that you have 5.00 liters of a gas at 1.00 atm pressure, and then you decrease the volume to 2.00 liters. What’s the new pressure?

To find the answer, follow these steps:

1. Use the following setup:
\[
P_1 V_1 = P_2 V_2
\]
2. Substitute 1.00 atmospheres for $P_1$, 5.00 liters for $V_1$, and 2.00 liters for $V_2$ and you get
\[
(1.00 \, \text{atm})(5.00 \, \text{L}) = P_2(2.00 \, \text{L})
\]
3. Solve for $P_2$:



## Charles’s Law

Charles’s law, named after Jacques Charles, a 19th-century French chemist, describes the relationship between volume and temperature while keeping pressure and the amount of gas constant. This law is commonly observed in everyday situations, particularly with the heating and cooling of balloons.

When a balloon is placed in a freezer or exposed to subzero temperatures, it shrinks. Although the external atmospheric pressure remains constant, the gas particles inside the balloon move slower at lower temperatures, resulting in a decrease in volume to maintain constant pressure. Conversely, when the balloon is heated, the gas particles move faster, causing the volume to increase as the balloon expands.

This relationship can be expressed mathematically as:

$$ \frac{V_1}{T_1} = \frac{V_2}{T_2} $$

where:
- \( V_1 \) and \( V_2 \) are the initial and final volumes,
- \( T_1 \) and \( T_2 \) are the initial and final temperatures in Kelvin.



Charles’s law states that the volume of a gas is directly proportional to its Kelvin temperature, expressed mathematically as:

\[
V \propto T
\]

This can be rewritten in equation form as:

\[
\frac{V_1}{T_1} = \frac{V_2}{T_2}
\]

where:
- \( V_1 \) is the initial volume,
- \( T_1 \) is the initial temperature in Kelvin,
- \( V_2 \) is the final volume,
- \( T_2 \) is the final temperature in Kelvin.

This relationship holds true when the pressure and the amount of gas are held constant. If any three of the quantities \( V_1 \), \( T_1 \), \( V_2 \), and \( T_2 \) are known, the fourth can be calculated.



```markdown
Suppose you live in Alaska and are outside in the middle of winter, where the temperature is –23 degrees Celsius. You blow up a balloon so that it has a volume of 1.00 liter. You then take it inside your home, where the temperature is a toasty 27 degrees Celsius. What’s the new volume of the balloon?

To solve, follow these steps:

1. Convert your temperatures to Kelvin by adding 273 to the Celsius temperature:
- $-23°C + 273 = 250K$ (outside)
- $27°C + 273 = 300K$ (inside)

2. Solve for $V_2$, using the following setup:

3. Multiply both sides by $T_2$ so that $V_2$ is on one side of the equation by itself:

4. Substitute the values to calculate the following answer:

This answer is reasonable, because Charles’s law says that if you increase the Kelvin temperature, the volume increases.

### Gay-Lussac’s Law

Gay-Lussac’s law, named after the 19th-century French scientist Joseph-Louis Gay-Lussac, deals with the relationship between the pressure and temperature of a gas if its volume and amount are held constant.
```



Gay-Lussac’s law states that the pressure of a gas is directly proportional to its Kelvin temperature when the volume is held constant. This relationship can be expressed mathematically as:

\[
\frac{P_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2}{T_2}
\]

where:
- \( P_1 \) is the initial pressure,
- \( T_1 \) is the initial temperature in Kelvin,
- \( P_2 \) is the final pressure,
- \( T_2 \) is the final temperature in Kelvin.

This equation illustrates how changes in temperature affect the pressure of a gas when the volume remains unchanged.



To find the new pressure when a gas is heated, you can use the combined gas law, which is derived from Boyle's law, Charles's law, and Gay-Lussac's law. The combined gas law is expressed as:

\[
\frac{P_1 V_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2 V_2}{T_2}
\]

Where:
- \(P_1\) = initial pressure
- \(V_1\) = initial volume
- \(T_1\) = initial temperature (in Kelvin)
- \(P_2\) = final pressure
- \(V_2\) = final volume
- \(T_2\) = final temperature (in Kelvin)

In the case where the volume remains constant, the equation simplifies to:

\[
\frac{P_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2}{T_2}
\]

To find the new pressure \(P_2\), rearrange the equation:

\[
P_2 = P_1 \cdot \frac{T_2}{T_1}
\]

Substituting the given values:

- \(P_1 = 800 \, \text{torr}\)
- \(T_1 = 250 \, \text{K}\)
- \(T_2 = 400 \, \text{K}\)

Calculating \(P_2\):

\[
P_2 = 800 \, \text{torr} \cdot \frac{400 \, \text{K}}{250 \, \text{K}} = 800 \, \text{torr} \cdot 1.6 = 1280 \, \text{torr}
\]

Thus, the new pressure is \(1280 \, \text{torr}\). This result is reasonable because heating the gas increases its pressure.



The combined gas law is expressed as:

\[
\frac{P_1 V_1}{T_1} = \frac{P_2 V_2}{T_2}
\]

Where:
- \( P \) is the pressure of the gas (in atm, mm Hg, torr, etc.),
- \( V \) is the volume of the gas (in appropriate units),
- \( T \) is the temperature (in Kelvin),
- The subscripts 1 and 2 refer to the initial and final conditions, respectively.

To find the new volume \( V_2 \), rearrange the combined gas law:

\[
V_2 = \frac{P_1 V_1 T_2}{P_2 T_1}
\]

### Example Problem

Given:
- Initial volume \( V_1 = 25.0 \, \text{L} \)
- Initial pressure \( P_1 = 1.00 \, \text{atm} \)
- Initial temperature \( T_1 = 27 \, \text{°C} = 300 \, \text{K} \) (convert to Kelvin by adding 273)
- Final pressure \( P_2 = 0.500 \, \text{atm} \)
- Final temperature \( T_2 = -33 \, \text{°C} = 240 \, \text{K} \) (convert to Kelvin)

Substituting the values into the rearranged equation:

\[
V_2 = \frac{(1.00 \, \text{atm}) (25.0 \, \text{L}) (240 \, \text{K})}{(0.500 \, \text{atm}) (300 \, \text{K})}
\]

Calculating \( V_2 \):

\[
V_2 = \frac{6000 \, \text{L} \cdot \text{K}}{150 \, \text{atm} \cdot \text{K}} = 40.0 \, \text{L}
\]

Thus, the new volume of the balloon at the higher altitude is \( 40.0 \, \text{L} \).



```markdown
2. Identify your quantities:
- \( P_1 = 1.00 \, \text{atm} \)
- \( V_1 = 25.0 \, \text{L} \)
- \( T_1 = 27^\circ \text{C} + 273 = 300 \, \text{K} \)
- \( P_2 = 0.500 \, \text{atm} \)
- \( T_2 = -33^\circ \text{C} + 273 = 240 \, \text{K} \)

3. Substitute the values to calculate the following answer:

- \( V_2 = 40.0 \, \text{L} \)

Because the volume increased overall in this case, Boyle’s law had a greater effect than Charles’s law.

### Avogadro’s Law

The combined gas equation gives you a way to calculate changes involving pressure, volume, and temperature. But you still have the problem of amount to deal with. In order to account for amount, you need to know another law.

Amedeo Avogadro (the same Avogadro that gave us his famous number of particles per mole) determined, from his study of gases, that equal volumes of gases at the same temperature and pressure contain equal numbers of gas particles. So Avogadro’s law says that the volume of a gas is directly proportional to the number of moles of gas (number of gas particles) at a constant temperature and pressure. Mathematically, Avogadro’s law looks like this:

\[
V = k n \quad (\text{at constant temperature and pressure})
\]

In this equation, \( k \) is a constant and \( n \) is the number of moles of gas.
```



A very useful consequence of Avogadro’s law is that the volume of a mole of gas can be calculated at any temperature and pressure. An extremely useful form to know when calculating the volume of a mole of gas is that 1 mole of any gas at STP occupies 22.4 liters. STP in this case stands for standard temperature and pressure.

- **Standard pressure**: 1.00 atm (760. torr or 760. mm Hg)
- **Standard temperature**: 273 K

This relationship between moles of gas and liters provides a way to convert the gas from a mass to a volume.

Suppose that you have 50.0 grams of oxygen gas ($\text{O}_2$), and you want to know its volume at STP.



You now know that the 50.0 grams of oxygen gas occupies a volume of 35.0 liters at STP. But what if the gas is not at STP?

What’s the volume of 50.0 grams of oxygen at 2.00 atm and 27.0 degrees Celsius?

In the next section, I show you a really easy way of doing this problem. But right now, you can use the combined gas law, because you know the volume at STP:

\[
P_1 = 1.00 \, \text{atm}, \quad V_1 = 35.0 \, \text{L}, \quad T_1 = 273 \, \text{K}
\]
\[
P_2 = 2.00 \, \text{atm}, \quad T_2 = 300 \, \text{K} \, (27°C + 273)
\]

Solving for \(V_2\), you calculate the following answer:

The ideal gas equation

If you take Boyle’s law, Charles’s law, Gay-Lussac’s law, and Avogadro’s law and throw them into a blender on high for a minute, you get the ideal gas equation — a way of working in volume, temperature, pressure, and amount. The ideal gas equation has the following form:

\[
PV = nRT
\]



The P represents pressure in atmospheres (atm), the V represents volume in liters (L), the n represents moles of gas, the T represents the temperature in Kelvin (K), and the R represents the ideal gas constant, which is 0.0821 liters atm/K mol.

Using this value of the ideal gas constant, the pressure must be expressed in atm, and the volume must be expressed in liters. You can calculate other ideal gas constants if you really want to use torr and milliliters, for example, but why bother? It’s easier to memorize one value for R and then remember to express the pressure and volume in the appropriate units. Naturally, you’ll always express the temperature in Kelvin when working any kind of gas law problem.

That said, now I want to show you an easy way to convert a gas from a mass to a volume if the gas is not at STP:

What’s the volume of 50.0 grams of oxygen at 2.00 atm and 27.0 degrees Celsius?

To answer this question, do the following:

1. Convert the 50.0 grams of oxygen to moles using the molecular weight of $O_2$:

2. Take the ideal gas equation and rearrange it so you can solve for $V$:
$$PV = nRT$$



## The van der Waals equation

The ideal gas equation is useful for gases that come close to obeying the kinetic molecular theory; in other words, an ideal gas obeys the ideal gas equation. However, in situations where the gas isn’t ideal and doesn’t obey the ideal gas equation—such as with polar gases (which have increased attractive forces) or high concentrations of gases (where the volume of the gas particles cannot be ignored)—the van der Waals equation is applicable.

Johannes van der Waals developed an equation that accounts for the attractive forces and the volumes of real gases by introducing two constants, \( a \) and \( b \), into the ideal gas equation. The values for the \( a \) and \( b \) constants for each individual gas can be found in the Chemical Rubber Company (CRC) Handbook. The resulting equation is called the van der Waals equation:

\[
\left( P + \frac{a n^2}{V^2} \right) (V - nb) = nRT
\]

In this equation:
- \( P \) is the pressure of the gas,
- \( V \) is the volume of the gas,
- \( n \) is the number of moles of the gas,
- \( R \) is the ideal gas constant,
- \( T \) is the temperature in Kelvin,
- \( a \) accounts for the attractive forces between gas particles,
- \( b \) accounts for the volume occupied by the gas particles.

The attraction of the gas particles for each other slightly reduces the pressure of the gas. The \( a \) constant, combined with the moles of gas, \( n \), adjusts for the attractive force: the greater the attractive force, the greater the value of \( a \).



The actual volume of a real gas is less than an ideal gas. The volume can be corrected by the $V - nb$ term. The larger the gas particle, the more volume it occupies and the larger its $b$ value.

### Applying Gas Laws to Stoichiometry

The ideal gas equation (and even the combined gas equation) allows chemists to work stoichiometry problems involving gases. In this section, you use the ideal gas equation to solve a problem, using a classic chemistry experiment — the decomposition of potassium chlorate to potassium chloride and oxygen by heating:

$$
2 \text{KClO}_3(s) \rightarrow 2 \text{KCl}(s) + 3 \text{O}_2(g)
$$

**To calculate the volume, do the following:**

1. Calculate the number of moles of oxygen gas produced.
2. Convert the temperature to Kelvin and the pressure to atmospheres:
- $27°C + 273 = 300 \, K$
- $\frac{700 \, \text{torr}}{760 \, \text{torr/atm}} = 0.9211 \, \text{atm}$
3. Put everything in the ideal gas equation:



# Tackling Dalton’s and Graham’s Laws

## Dalton’s Law

Dalton’s law of partial pressures states that in a mixture of gases, the total pressure is the sum of the partial pressures of each individual gas.

If you have a mixture of gases — gas A, gas B, gas C, and so on — then the total pressure of the system can be expressed mathematically as:

\[
P_{\text{total}} = P_A + P_B + P_C + \ldots
\]

When working on stoichiometry problems, such as the decomposition of potassium chlorate, the oxygen is typically collected over water by displacement, and the volume is then measured. However, to obtain the pressure of just the oxygen, you must account for the vapor pressure of water.



```markdown
Subtract the pressure due to the water vapor. You have to mathematically “dry out” the gas.

Suppose, for example, that a sample of oxygen is collected over water at a total pressure of 755 torr at 20 degrees Celsius. Your first task is to reference a table of vapor pressures of water versus temperature. After looking at the table, you determine that the partial pressure of water at 20 degrees Celsius is 17.5 torr. Now you’re ready to calculate the pressure of the oxygen:

\[
P_{\text{total}} = P_{\text{oxygen}} + P_{\text{water vapor}}
\]
\[
755 \, \text{torr} = P_{\text{oxygen}} + 17.5 \, \text{torr}
\]
\[
P_{\text{oxygen}} = 755 \, \text{torr} - 17.5 \, \text{torr} = 737.5 \, \text{torr}
\]

Knowing the partial pressure of gases like oxygen is important in deep-sea diving and the operation of respirators in hospitals.

### Graham’s Law

Place a few drops of a strong perfume on a table at one end of a room, and soon people at the other end of the room can smell it. This process is called gaseous diffusion, the mixing of gases due to their molecular motion.

Place a few drops of that same perfume inside an ordinary rubber balloon and blow it up. Very soon you’ll be able to smell it.
```



The perfume outside of the balloon as it makes its way through the microscopic pores of the rubber. This process is called gaseous effusion, the movement of a gas through a tiny opening. The same process of effusion is responsible for the helium being quickly lost from rubber balloons.

Thomas Graham determined that the rates or velocities ($v$) of diffusion and effusion of gases are inversely proportional to the square roots of their molecular or atomic weights ($M$). This relationship is called Graham’s law. In general, it says that the lighter the gas, the faster it will effuse (or diffuse). Mathematically, Graham’s law looks like this:

\[
\frac{v_1}{v_2} = \sqrt{\frac{M_2}{M_1}}
\]

The concept of diffusion can be easily demonstrated by using latex balloons and some flavoring extract. Place a few drops of an extract (vanilla, peppermint, almond, or whatever you like) inside a balloon. Then inflate the balloon, tie it off, and shake it. After a few minutes, smell the balloon. You should be able to detect the specific aroma of the extract you placed inside the balloon.

The extracts are fairly volatile substances, and they vaporize inside the balloon and diffuse through the pores in the latex to our noses. Some chemists believe that the extract molecules may actually interact with the latex, making penetration of the balloon walls easier.

Suppose that you fill two rubber balloons to the same size, one with hydrogen ($H_2$) and the other with oxygen ($O_2$). The hydrogen, being lighter, should effuse through the balloon pores faster than the oxygen.



Using Graham’s law, you can determine the rate of effusion of gases. The law states that the rate of effusion of a gas is inversely proportional to the square root of its molar mass. Mathematically, this can be expressed as:

\[
\frac{r_1}{r_2} = \sqrt{\frac{M_2}{M_1}}
\]

where:
- \( r_1 \) and \( r_2 \) are the rates of effusion of gas 1 and gas 2, respectively,
- \( M_1 \) and \( M_2 \) are the molar masses of gas 1 and gas 2, respectively.

For example, if we consider hydrogen (H₂) and oxygen (O₂), the molar masses are approximately:
- \( M_{H_2} = 2 \, \text{g/mol} \)
- \( M_{O_2} = 32 \, \text{g/mol} \)

Using Graham’s law, we can calculate the ratio of their effusion rates:

\[
\frac{r_{H_2}}{r_{O_2}} = \sqrt{\frac{M_{O_2}}{M_{H_2}}} = \sqrt{\frac{32}{2}} = \sqrt{16} = 4
\]

This means that hydrogen should effuse out four times as fast as oxygen.

A Cornucopia of Chemical
        Concepts



In this part . . .



```markdown
## Chemical Reactions

Chemistry is fundamentally about chemical reactions, which are used to create new substances such as drugs, plastics, and cleaners. These reactions are essential for analyzing samples to determine their composition and quantities. Chemical reactions are also responsible for the processes that power our bodies, the sun, and the universe.

### The Mole

The mole is a key concept in chemistry that bridges the microscopic world of atoms and molecules with the macroscopic world of grams and metric tons. Understanding the mole is crucial for quantifying substances in chemical reactions.

### Solutions

Solutions are homogeneous mixtures of two or more substances. To prepare a solution, one must know the solute and solvent involved, as well as the desired concentration. Concentration can be expressed in various ways, including molarity (M), which is defined as:

$$
M = \frac{\text{moles of solute}}{\text{liters of solution}}
$$

### Thermochemistry

Thermochemistry studies the heat changes that occur during chemical reactions. Reactions can either release energy (exothermic) or absorb energy (endothermic). The heat change ($\Delta H$) can be calculated using the formula:

$$
\Delta H = H_{\text{products}} - H_{\text{reactants}}
$$

### Acids and Bases

Acids and bases have distinct properties. Acids typically have a sour taste and can donate protons (H$^+$ ions), while bases have a bitter taste and can accept protons. The strength of an acid or base is often measured using the pH scale, which is defined as:

$$
\text{pH} = -\log[H^+]
$$

A pH less than 7 indicates an acidic solution, while a pH greater than 7 indicates a basic solution.
```



# Chapter 7: Chemical Cooking: Chemical Reactions

## Knowing What You Have and What You’ll Get: Reactants and Products

Chemists do a lot of things: They measure the physical properties of substances, they analyze mixtures to find out what they’re composed of, and they make new substances. The process of making chemical compounds is called synthesis, and it depends on chemical reactions.

In this chapter, I discuss chemical reactions — how they occur, the different types of chemical reactions, and how to write a balanced chemical equation.

### Differentiating between Reactants and Products

In a chemical reaction, the substances that undergo change are called reactants, while the substances formed as a result of the reaction are called products.

### Applying Energy to Set Off Reactions

Chemical reactions often require energy to initiate. This energy can come in various forms, such as heat, light, or electricity.

### Taking a Look at Different Kinds of Reactions

There are several types of chemical reactions, including:

1. **Synthesis Reactions**: Two or more reactants combine to form a single product.
\[
A + B \rightarrow AB
\]

2. **Decomposition Reactions**: A single compound breaks down into two or more products.
\[
AB \rightarrow A + B
\]

3. **Single Replacement Reactions**: An element replaces another element in a compound.
\[
A + BC \rightarrow AC + B
\]

4. **Double Replacement Reactions**: The ions of two compounds exchange places in an aqueous solution to form two new compounds.
\[
AB + CD \rightarrow AD + CB
\]

5. **Combustion Reactions**: A substance combines with oxygen, releasing energy in the form of light or heat.
\[
C_xH_y + O_2 \rightarrow CO_2 + H_2O
\]

### Keeping Track of Atoms with Balanced Reactions

To ensure that the law of conservation of mass is upheld, chemical equations must be balanced. This means that the number of atoms of each element must be the same on both sides of the equation.

For example, in the reaction:
\[
2H_2 + O_2 \rightarrow 2H_2O
\]
There are 4 hydrogen atoms and 2 oxygen atoms on both sides of the equation, thus it is balanced.

Balancing chemical equations involves adjusting the coefficients (the numbers in front of the compounds) to achieve the same number of each type of atom on both sides of the equation.



In a chemical reaction, substances (elements and/or compounds) are changed into other substances (compounds and/or elements). You can’t change one element into another in a chemical reaction — that happens in nuclear reactions. Instead, you create new substances with chemical reactions.

A number of clues show that a chemical reaction has taken place — something new is visibly produced, a gas is created, heat is given off or taken in, and so on. The chemical substances that are eventually changed are called the reactants, and the new substances that are formed are called the products. Chemical equations show the reactants and products, as well as other factors such as energy changes, catalysts, and so on. With these equations, an arrow is used to indicate that a chemical reaction has taken place. In general terms, a chemical reaction follows this format:

$$\text{Reactants} \rightarrow \text{Products}$$

For example, when you light your natural gas range to fry your breakfast eggs, methane (natural gas) reacts with the oxygen in the atmosphere to produce carbon dioxide and water vapor. The chemical equation that represents this reaction is written as:

$$\text{CH}_4(g) + 2 \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow \text{CO}_2(g) + 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}(g)$$

This equation indicates that one molecule of methane gas, $\text{CH}_4(g)$, reacts with two molecules of oxygen gas, $\text{O}_2(g)$, to produce carbon dioxide and water vapor.



In the combustion of methane, the reaction can be represented as follows:

$$
\text{CH}_4(g) + 2 \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow \text{CO}_2(g) + 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}(g)
$$

In this reaction, methane ($\text{CH}_4$) and oxygen ($\text{O}_2$) are the reactants, while carbon dioxide ($\text{CO}_2$) and water ($\text{H}_2\text{O}$) are the products. The coefficients indicate the number of molecules involved in the reaction: one molecule of methane, two molecules of oxygen, one molecule of carbon dioxide, and two molecules of water.

This reaction is an example of an exothermic reaction, where heat is released. In contrast, endothermic reactions absorb energy. For instance, cooking involves many endothermic reactions, such as frying eggs, which require heat to initiate the chemical changes.

To initiate the combustion of methane, an external energy source, known as activation energy, is required. This can be provided by a match, lighter, or electric igniter. Activation energy is necessary for both exothermic and endothermic reactions, although it is more apparent in exothermic reactions.



# Understanding How Reactions Occur: The Collision Theory

In order for a chemical reaction to take place, the reactants must collide. Energy is required to break a bond between atoms, and energy is released when a bond is made. The collision theory states that the collision between the molecules can provide the energy needed to break the necessary bonds so that new bonds can be formed. The collision must occur at the right spot and transfer sufficient energy.

## One-Step Collision Example

When a collision occurs, it may not always result in a reaction. Sometimes, even if a collision takes place, not enough kinetic energy is available to be transferred — the molecules aren’t moving fast enough. Heating the mixture of reactants can increase the average kinetic energy of the molecules, potentially leading to successful collisions.



Raising the temperature increases the kinetic energy available to break bonds during collisions.

Sometimes, even if you hit the ball hard enough, it doesn’t go into the pocket because you didn’t hit it in the right spot. The same is true during a molecular collision. The molecules must collide in the right orientation, or hit at the right spot, in order for the reaction to occur.

Suppose you have an equation showing molecule A-B reacting with C to form C-A and B, like this:

$$
\text{A-B} + \text{C} \rightarrow \text{C-A} + \text{B}
$$

The way this equation is written, the reaction requires that reactant C collide with A-B on the A end of the molecule. (You know this because the product side shows C hooked up with A — C-A.) If it hits the B end, nothing happens. The A end of this hypothetical molecule is called the reactive site, the place on the molecule that the collision must take place in order for the reaction to occur. If C collides at the A end of the molecule, then enough energy may be transferred to break the A-B bond. After the A-B bond is broken, the C-A bond can be formed. The equation for this reaction process can be shown in this way (I show the breaking of the A-B bond and the forming of the C-A bond as “squiggly” bonds):

$$
\text{C} \sim \text{A} \sim \text{B} \rightarrow \text{C-A} + \text{B}
$$

So in order for this reaction to occur, a collision between C and A-B must occur at the reactive site. The collision between C and A-B has to transfer enough energy to break the A-B bond, allowing the C-A bond to form.



If instead of having a simple A-B molecule, you have a large complex molecule, like a protein or a polymer, then the likelihood of C colliding at the reactive site is much smaller. You may have a lot of collisions, but few at the reactive site. This reaction will probably be much slower than the simple case.

Note that this example is a simple one. I’ve assumed that only one collision is needed, making this a one-step reaction. Many reactions are one-step, but many others require several steps before the reactants become the final products. In the process, several compounds may be formed that react with each other to give the final products. These compounds are called intermediates. They’re shown in the reaction mechanism, the series of steps that the reaction goes through in going from reactants to products. But in this chapter, I keep it simple and pretty much limit my discussion to one-step reactions.

### Considering an exothermic example

Imagine that the hypothetical reaction

$$ A-B + C \rightarrow C-A + B $$

is exothermic — a reaction in which thermal energy is given off (released) when going from reactants to products. The reactants start off at a higher energy state than the products, so energy is released in going from reactants to products.

In this reaction, $E_a$ is the activation energy for the reaction — the energy that you have to put in to get the reaction going. The collision of C and A-B leads to the breaking of the A-B bond and the forming of the C-A bond at the top of an activation energy hill. This grouping of reactants at the top of the activation energy hill is sometimes called the transition state of the reaction. The difference in the energy level of the reactants and the energy level of the products is the amount of energy (heat) that is released in the reaction.



```markdown
Some reactions may give off energy but not thermal energy. An example is light sticks. You mix two chemical solutions by flexing the light stick, and the resulting product glows — it gives off light but not heat. Another example is fireflies, which mix two chemicals in their bodies and give off light. These reactions that give off energy are exergonic. If that energy is in the form of heat, the reaction is subclassified as exothermic.

### Endothermic Reactions

Suppose that the hypothetical reaction

$$ A-B + C \rightarrow C-A + B $$

is endothermic — a reaction in which heat is absorbed — so the reactants are at a lower energy state than the products. This diagram shows that an activation energy is required for the reaction to proceed.
```



In going from reactants to products, you have to put in more energy initially to get the reaction started, and then you get some of that energy back out as the reaction proceeds. Notice that the transition state appears at the top of the activation energy hill — just like in the exothermic-reaction energy diagram. But although both endothermic and exothermic reactions require activation energy, exothermic reactions release thermal energy, and endothermic reactions absorb it.

Cooking is a great example of an endothermic reaction. That ground beef isn’t going to be a delicious hamburger unless you cook it. You have to continually supply energy in order for the chemical reactions called cooking to take place. Cold packs that athletic trainers use to treat injuries are another example of an endothermic reaction. Two solutions in the pack are mixed, and the pack absorbs thermal energy from the surroundings. The surroundings therefore become colder.

Endothermic reaction of $A + B \rightarrow C - A + B$.



## Identifying Different Types of Reactions

Several general types of chemical reactions can occur based on what happens when going from reactants to products. The more common reactions are:

- Combination
- Decomposition
- Single displacement
- Double displacement
- Combustion
- Redox

### Combination Reactions

In combination reactions, two or more reactants form one product. For example, the reaction of sodium and chlorine to form sodium chloride can be represented as:

$$
2 \text{Na} + \text{Cl}_2 \rightarrow 2 \text{NaCl}
$$

### Decomposition Reactions

In decomposition reactions, a single compound breaks down into two or more simpler products. An example is the decomposition of water into hydrogen and oxygen:

$$
2 \text{H}_2\text{O} \rightarrow 2 \text{H}_2 + \text{O}_2
$$

### Single Displacement Reactions

In single displacement reactions, one element displaces another in a compound. For example, when zinc reacts with hydrochloric acid, zinc displaces hydrogen:

$$
\text{Zn} + 2 \text{HCl} \rightarrow \text{ZnCl}_2 + \text{H}_2
$$

### Double Displacement Reactions

In double displacement reactions, the anions and cations of two different compounds exchange places to form two new compounds. An example is the reaction between silver nitrate and sodium chloride:

$$
\text{AgNO}_3 + \text{NaCl} \rightarrow \text{AgCl} + \text{NaNO}_3
$$

### Combustion Reactions

Combustion reactions involve the reaction of a substance with oxygen, producing energy in the form of heat and light. A common example is the combustion of methane:

$$
\text{CH}_4 + 2 \text{O}_2 \rightarrow \text{CO}_2 + 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}
$$

### Redox Reactions

Redox reactions involve the transfer of electrons between two species. An example is the reaction between iron and copper(II) sulfate:

$$
\text{Fe} + \text{CuSO}_4 \rightarrow \text{FeSO}_4 + \text{Cu}
$$

In this reaction, iron is oxidized and copper is reduced.



```markdown
## Combination Reactions

Combination reactions involve two or more substances combining to form a single product. Examples include:

- The reaction of sodium and chlorine:
$$ 2 \text{Na}(s) + \text{Cl}_2(g) \rightarrow 2 \text{NaCl}(s) $$

- The burning of coal (carbon) to produce carbon dioxide:
$$ \text{C}(s) + \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow \text{CO}_2(g) $$

In combination reactions, depending on the conditions or the relative amounts of the reactants, more than one product can be formed. For instance, in the burning of coal, if there is an excess of oxygen, the product is carbon dioxide. However, if there is a limited amount of oxygen available, the product is carbon monoxide:
$$ 2 \text{C}(s) + \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow 2 \text{CO}(g) \quad \text{(limited oxygen)} $$

## Decomposition Reactions

Decomposition reactions are the opposite of combination reactions. In these reactions, a single compound breaks down into two or more simpler substances (elements and/or compounds). Examples include:

- The decomposition of water into hydrogen and oxygen gases:
$$ 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}(l) \rightarrow 2 \text{H}_2(g) + \text{O}_2(g) $$

- The decomposition of hydrogen peroxide to form oxygen gas and water:
$$ 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}_2(l) \rightarrow 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}(l) + \text{O}_2(g) $$
```



# Single-displacement Reactions

In single-displacement reactions, a more active element displaces (kicks out) another less active element from a compound. For example, if you put a piece of zinc metal into a copper(II) sulfate solution, the zinc displaces the copper, as shown in this equation:

$$
\text{Zn}(s) + \text{CuSO}_4(aq) \rightarrow \text{ZnSO}_4(aq) + \text{Cu}(s)
$$

The notation (aq) indicates that the compound is dissolved in water — in an aqueous solution. Because zinc replaces copper in this case, it’s said to be more active. If you place a piece of copper in a zinc sulfate solution, nothing happens.

An activity table lists the activity of metals. The activity series of some common metals is as follows:

- Most active: Alkali and alkaline earth metals
- Al
- Zn
- Cr
- Fe
- Ni
- Sn
- Pb



```markdown
Take another look at the reaction between zinc metal and copper(II) sulfate solution:

$$ \text{Zn}(s) + \text{CuSO}_4(aq) \rightarrow \text{ZnSO}_4(aq) + \text{Cu}(s) $$

I’ve written this reaction as a molecular equation, showing all species in the molecular/atomic form. However, these reactions normally occur in an aqueous (water) solution. When the ionically bonded CuSO4 is dissolved in water, it breaks apart into ions (atoms or groups of atoms that have an electrical charge due to the loss or gain of electrons). The copper ion has a +2 charge because it lost two electrons. It’s a cation, a positively charged ion. The sulfate ion has a –2 charge because it has two extra electrons. It’s an anion, a negatively charged ion.

To show the reaction and production of ions in a reaction, you can write an ionic equation, like the following:

Notice that the sulfate ion, $\text{SO}_4^{2-}$, doesn’t change in the reaction. Ions that don’t change during the reaction and are found on both sides of the equation in an identical form are called spectator ions. Chemists often omit the spectator ions and write the equation showing only those chemical substances that are changed during the reaction. This format is called the net-ionic equation.
```



## Double-displacement Reactions

In double-displacement reactions, or metathesis reactions, two species (normally ions) are displaced. Most of the time, reactions of this type occur in a solution, and either an insoluble solid (precipitation reactions) or water (neutralization reactions) is formed.

### Precipitation Reactions

If you mix a solution of potassium chloride and a solution of silver nitrate, a white insoluble solid is formed in the resulting solution. The formation of an insoluble solid in a solution is called precipitation. Here are the molecular, ionic, and net-ionic equations for this double-displacement reaction:

1. **Molecular Equation:**
\[
\text{KCl (aq) + AgNO}_3\text{ (aq) } \rightarrow \text{AgCl (s) + KNO}_3\text{ (aq)}
\]

2. **Ionic Equation:**
\[
\text{K}^+\text{ (aq) + Cl}^-\text{ (aq) + Ag}^+\text{ (aq) + NO}_3^-\text{ (aq) } \rightarrow \text{AgCl (s) + K}^+\text{ (aq) + NO}_3^-\text{ (aq)}
\]

3. **Net-Ionic Equation:**
\[
\text{Ag}^+\text{ (aq) + Cl}^-\text{ (aq) } \rightarrow \text{AgCl (s)}
\]

The white insoluble solid that’s formed is silver chloride. The potassium cation and nitrate anion are spectator ions, as they don’t change during the reaction and are found on both sides of the equation in an identical form.



In order to write these equations, you have to know something about the solubility of ionic compounds. If a compound is soluble, it will remain in its free ion form, but if it’s insoluble, it will precipitate (form a solid).

To use the solubility rules, take the cation of one reactant and combine it with the anion of the other reactant, and vice versa (keeping the neutrality of the compounds). This allows you to predict the possible products of the reaction. Then look up the solubility of the possible products. If the compound is insoluble, it precipitates. If it is soluble, it remains in solution.

### Neutralization Reactions

The other type of double-displacement reaction is the reaction between an acid and a base. This double-displacement reaction, called a neutralization reaction, forms water.

For example, consider the mixing of solutions of sulfuric acid ($\text{H}_2\text{SO}_4$) and sodium hydroxide ($\text{NaOH}$). The molecular, ionic, and net-ionic equations for this reaction are as follows:

1. **Molecular Equation**:
\[
\text{H}_2\text{SO}_4(aq) + 2\text{NaOH}(aq) \rightarrow \text{Na}_2\text{SO}_4(aq) + 2\text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
\]

2. **Ionic Equation**:
\[
\text{H}^+(aq) + \text{SO}_4^{2-}(aq) + 2\text{Na}^+(aq) + 2\text{OH}^-(aq) \rightarrow \text{Na}^+(aq) + \text{SO}_4^{2-}(aq) + 2\text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
\]

3. **Net-Ionic Equation**:
\[
\text{H}^+(aq) + \text{OH}^-(aq) \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
\]



```markdown
### Acid-Base Reactions

The molecular equation for the reaction between sulfuric acid and sodium hydroxide is:

$$
\text{H}_2\text{SO}_4(aq) + 2 \text{NaOH}(aq) \rightarrow \text{Na}_2\text{SO}_4(aq) + 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
$$

The corresponding ionic equation is:

$$
2 \text{H}^+(aq) + \text{SO}_4^{2-}(aq) + 2 \text{Na}^+(aq) + 2 \text{OH}^-(aq) \rightarrow 2 \text{Na}^+(aq) + \text{SO}_4^{2-}(aq) + 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
$$

The net-ionic equation, which excludes the spectator ions, is:

$$
2 \text{H}^+(aq) + 2 \text{OH}^-(aq) \rightarrow 2 \text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
$$

or simplified to:

$$
\text{H}^+(aq) + \text{OH}^-(aq) \rightarrow \text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
$$

### Combustion Reactions

Combustion reactions occur when a compound, usually one containing carbon, combines with oxygen gas in the air. This process is commonly called burning. Heat is the most useful product of most combustion reactions.

The molecular equation that represents the burning of propane is:

$$
\text{C}_3\text{H}_8(g) + 5 \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow 3 \text{CO}_2(g) + 4 \text{H}_2\text{O}(l)
$$

Propane belongs to a class of compounds called hydrocarbons, which are composed only of carbon and hydrogen. The product of this reaction is heat.
```



# Redox Reactions

Redox reactions, or reduction-oxidation reactions, are reactions in which electrons are exchanged:

\[
2 \text{Na}(s) + \text{Cl}_2(g) \rightarrow 2 \text{NaCl}(s)
\]
\[
\text{C}(s) + \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow \text{CO}_2(g)
\]
\[
\text{Zn}(s) + \text{CuSO}_4(aq) \rightarrow \text{ZnSO}_4(aq) + \text{Cu}(s)
\]

The preceding reactions are examples of other types of reactions (such as combination, combustion, and single-replacement reactions), but they’re all also redox reactions. They all involve the transfer of electrons from one chemical species to another. Redox reactions are involved in combustion, rusting, photosynthesis, respiration, batteries, and more.

# Balancing Chemical Reactions

If you carry out a chemical reaction, carefully sum up the masses of all the reactants, and compare that sum to the sum of the masses of all the products, you see that they’re the same. In fact, a law in chemistry, the law of conservation of mass, states, “In an ordinary chemical reaction, matter is neither created nor destroyed.” This means that you have neither gained nor lost any atoms during the reaction. They may be combined differently, but they’re still there.

A chemical equation represents the reaction. That chemical equation is used to calculate how much of each element is involved in the reaction.



## Balancing Chemical Equations

To balance a chemical equation, you must ensure that the number of each type of atom is the same on both sides of the equation, adhering to the law of conservation of mass.

### Balancing Ammonia Production

One important reaction is the Haber process, which produces ammonia ($\text{NH}_3$) by reacting nitrogen gas ($\text{N}_2$) with hydrogen gas ($\text{H}_2$):

$$
\text{N}_2(g) + \text{H}_2(g) \rightarrow \text{NH}_3(g)
$$

This equation illustrates the reaction but does not indicate the quantities of each element required to produce ammonia. To determine the necessary amounts, the equation must be balanced, ensuring that the number of atoms on the left side equals the number on the right.

In this reaction, the reactants and product are fixed; you cannot alter the compounds or their subscripts, as that would change the identity of the compounds. The only method to balance the equation is by adding coefficients, which are whole numbers placed in front of the compounds or elements. These coefficients indicate the number of atoms or molecules present.



Each water molecule is composed of two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom. So with 2 H2O, you have a total of 4 hydrogen atoms and 2 oxygen atoms:

\[
2 \text{ H}_2\text{O} = \text{H}_2\text{O} + \text{H}_2\text{O}
\]

In this chapter, I show you how to balance equations by using a method called balancing by inspection, or as I call it, “fiddling with coefficients.” You take each atom in turn and balance it by adding appropriate coefficients to one side or the other.

Take another look at the equation for preparing ammonia:

\[
\text{N}_2(g) + \text{H}_2(g) \rightarrow \text{NH}_3(g)
\]

In most cases, waiting until the end to balance hydrogen atoms or oxygen atoms is a good idea based on many years of experience; balance the other atoms first.

So in this example, you need to balance the nitrogen atoms first. You have 2 nitrogen atoms on the left side of the arrow (reactant side) and only 1 nitrogen atom on the right side (product side). In order to balance the nitrogen atoms, use a coefficient of 2 in front of the ammonia on the right.

\[
\text{N}_2(g) + \text{H}_2(g) \rightarrow 2 \text{NH}_3(g)
\]



Now you have 2 nitrogen atoms on the left and 2 nitrogen atoms on the right.

Next, tackle the hydrogen atoms. You have 2 hydrogen atoms on the left and 6 hydrogen atoms on the right (2 NH3 molecules, each with 3 hydrogen atoms, for a total of 6 hydrogen atoms). So put a 3 in front of the H2 on the left, giving you:

$$
\text{N}_2(g) + 3 \text{H}_2(g) \rightarrow 2 \text{NH}_3(g)
$$

That should do it. Do a check to be sure: You have 2 nitrogen atoms on the left and 2 nitrogen atoms on the right. You have 6 hydrogen atoms on the left (3 × 2 = 6) and 6 hydrogen atoms on the right (2 × 3 = 6). The equation is balanced. You can read the equation this way: 1 nitrogen molecule reacts with 3 hydrogen molecules to yield 2 ammonia molecules.

This equation also balances with coefficients of 2, 6, and 4 instead of 1, 3, and 2. In fact, any multiple of 1, 3, and 2 balances the equation, but chemists always show the lowest whole-number ratio.

In this section, follow the steps to balance the equation showing the burning of butane, a hydrocarbon, with excess oxygen available. (This is the reaction that takes place when you light a butane lighter.)

1. Start with the unbalanced reaction written in a molecular equation.



```markdown
The equation of burning butane with excess oxygen available is:

$$
\text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}(g) + \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow \text{CO}_2(g) + \text{H}_2\text{O}(g)
$$

1. Balance the carbon atoms first. You have 4 carbon atoms on the left and 1 carbon atom on the right, so add a coefficient of 4 in front of the carbon dioxide:

$$
\text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}(g) + \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow 4 \text{CO}_2(g) + \text{H}_2\text{O}(g)
$$

2. When all non-hydrogen and non-oxygen atoms are balanced, balance the hydrogen atoms. You have 10 hydrogen atoms on the left and 2 hydrogen atoms on the right, so use a coefficient of 5 in front of the water on the right:

$$
\text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}(g) + \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow 4 \text{CO}_2(g) + 5 \text{H}_2\text{O}(g)
$$

3. Balance the oxygen atoms. You have 2 oxygen atoms on the left and a total of 13 oxygen atoms on the right [(4 × 2) + (5 × 1) = 13]. What can you multiply 2 with in order for it to equal 13? How about 6.5?

$$
\text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}(g) + 6.5 \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow 4 \text{CO}_2(g) + 5 \text{H}_2\text{O}(g)
$$

4. Multiply all coefficients in the equation to get the lowest whole-number ratio of coefficients. For this example, multiply the entire equation by 2:

$$
[ \text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}(g) + 6.5 \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow 4 \text{CO}_2(g) + 5 \text{H}_2\text{O}(g) ] \times 2
$$

Multiplying every coefficient by 2 gives:

$$
2 \text{C}_4\text{H}_{10}(g) + 13 \text{O}_2(g) \rightarrow 8 \text{CO}_2(g) + 10 \text{H}_2\text{O}(g)
$$
```



Most simple reactions can be balanced by checking the atom count on both sides of the equation to ensure that the equation is balanced and the coefficients are in the lowest whole-number ratio.

However, one class of reactions, redox reactions, is so complex that this method doesn’t work well when applied to them. A special method is used for balancing these equations.



# Chapter 8: The Mole: Can You Dig It?

One of the many things chemists do is make new substances, a process called synthesis. A logical question they ask is “How much?”

“How much of this reactant do I need to make this much product?” “How much product can I make with this much reactant?” In order to answer these questions, chemists must be able to take a balanced chemical equation, expressed in terms of atoms and molecules, and convert it to grams or pounds or tons — some type of unit they can actually weigh out in the lab. The mole concept enables chemists to move from the microscopic world of atoms and molecules to the real world of grams and kilograms and is one of the most important central concepts in chemistry.

## Counting Particles by Massing

Suppose that you have a job packing 1,000 nuts and 1,000 bolts in big bags, and you get paid for each bag you fill.



In chemistry, you count very large numbers of particles, such as atoms and molecules. To count them efficiently and quickly, you need to use the count-by-massing method, which means you need to know the masses of individual atoms and molecules. You can get the mass of the individual atoms on the periodic table, but what about the mass of the compounds? You can simply add together the masses of the individual atoms in the compound to figure the molecular mass or formula mass. (Molecular masses refer to covalently bonded compounds, and formula masses refer to both ionic and covalent compounds.)

Water, $H_2O$, is composed of two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom. By looking on the periodic table, you can find that one hydrogen atom equals 1.0079 amu and one oxygen atom weighs 15.999 amu (amu stands for atomic mass units). To calculate the molecular mass of water, you simply add together the atomic masses of two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom:

\[
2 \times 1.0079 \, \text{amu} = 2.016 \, \text{amu} \quad (\text{two hydrogen atoms})
\]
\[
1 \times 15.999 \, \text{amu} = 15.999 \, \text{amu} \quad (\text{one oxygen atom})
\]



```markdown
2.016 amu + 15.999 amu = 18.015 amu (the mass of a water molecule)

Now try a little harder one. Calculate the formula mass of aluminum sulfate, Al₂(SO₄)₃. In this salt, you have 2 aluminum atoms, 3 sulfur atoms, and 12 oxygen atoms. After you find the individual masses of the atoms on the periodic table, you can calculate the formula mass like this:

\[
(2 \, \text{Al} \times 26.982 \, \text{amu/Al}) + (3 \, \text{S} \times 32.066 \, \text{amu/S}) + (12 \, \text{O} \times 15.999 \, \text{amu/O}) = 342.150 \, \text{amu for Al}_2(\text{SO}_4)_3
\]

Using Moles to Count

When people deal with objects, they often think in terms of a convenient amount. For example, when a woman buys earrings, she normally buys a pair of them. When a man goes to the grocery store, he buys eggs by the dozen. And when I go to the office supply store, I buy copy paper by the ream.

People use words to represent numbers all the time — a pair is 2, a dozen is 12, and a ream is 500. All these words are units of measure, and they’re convenient for the objects they’re used to measure. Rarely would you buy a ream of earrings or a pair of paper.

Likewise, when chemists deal with atoms and molecules, they need a convenient unit that takes into consideration the very small size of atoms and molecules. The mole is just such a unit. The following sections dive deeper into how to count with a mole.
```



# Avogadro’s Number and the Mole

The word mole stands for a number — $6.022 \times 10^{23}$, commonly called Avogadro’s number, named for Amedeo Avogadro, the scientist who laid the groundwork for the mole principle.

A mole — $6.022 \times 10^{23}$ — is a really big number. When written in longhand notation, it’s

602,200,000,000,000,000,000,000.

If you had a mole of marshmallows, it would cover the United States to a depth of about 600 miles. A mole of rice grains would cover the land area of the world to a depth of about 75 meters.

Avogadro’s number stands for a certain number of things, typically atoms and molecules. The mole relates to the microscopic world of atoms and molecules, but it also relates to the macroscopic world.

A mole (abbreviated as mol) is the number of particles in exactly 12 grams of a particular isotope of carbon (C-12). Therefore, if you have exactly 12 grams of $^{12}\text{C}$, you have $6.022 \times 10^{23}$ carbon atoms, which is also a mole of $^{12}\text{C}$ atoms. For any other element, a mole is the atomic mass expressed in grams. For a compound, a mole is the formula (or molecular) mass in grams.



# Putting Moles to Work

The weight of a water molecule is 18.015 amu. Because a mole is the formula (or molecular) mass in grams of a compound, the mass of a mole of water is 18.015 grams. This means that 18.015 grams of water contains $6.022 \times 10^{23}$ H₂O molecules, or a mole of water. The mole of water is composed of two moles of hydrogen and one mole of oxygen.

The mole is the bridge between the microscopic and macroscopic worlds.

$$
6.022 \times 10^{23} \text{ particles} \leftrightarrow \text{ mole} \leftrightarrow \text{ atomic/formula/molecular mass in grams}
$$

If you have any one of the three things — particles, moles, or grams — you can calculate the other two.

### Example Problems

1. To find how many water molecules are in 5.50 moles of water, you can set up a calculation based on the relationship between moles and molecules.

2. To find how many moles are in 25.0 grams of water, you can set up a calculation using the mass of a mole of water.



To solve the problem of how many molecules are in 100.0 grams of carbon dioxide (CO₂), follow these steps:

1. **Determine the molecular mass of CO₂**:
- From the periodic table, the atomic masses are:
- Carbon (C): 12.011 amu
- Oxygen (O): 15.999 amu

2. **Calculate the molecular mass**:
\[
\text{Molecular mass of CO₂} = (1 \times 12.011 \, \text{g/mol}) + (2 \times 15.999 \, \text{g/mol}) = 44.01 \, \text{g/mol}
\]

3. **Convert grams to moles**:
\[
\text{Moles of CO₂} = \frac{100.0 \, \text{g}}{44.01 \, \text{g/mol}} \approx 2.27 \, \text{mol}
\]

4. **Convert moles to molecules**:
\[
\text{Number of molecules} = 2.27 \, \text{mol} \times 6.022 \times 10^{23} \, \text{molecules/mol} \approx 1.37 \times 10^{24} \, \text{molecules}
\]

### Calculating Empirical Formulas

To calculate the empirical formula of a compound using percentage composition data, follow these steps:

1. **Convert percentage to grams** (assuming 100 g of the compound).
2. **Convert grams to moles** using the molar mass of each element.
3. **Divide by the smallest number of moles** to find the simplest ratio.
4. **Write the empirical formula** based on the ratio of moles of each element.

The empirical formula represents the lowest whole-number ratio of each type of atom in the molecule.



```markdown
Percentage composition is one of the first analyses that a chemist does in learning about a new compound. For example, suppose I determine that a particular compound has the following weight percentage of elements present:

- 26.4% Na
- 36.8% S
- 36.8% O

Because I’m dealing with percentage data (amount per hundred), I assume that I have 100 grams of the compound so that my percentages can be used as masses. I then convert each mass to moles, like this:

Now I can write an empirical formula:

$$ \text{Na}_{1.15}\text{S}_{1.15}\text{O}_{2.30} $$

I know that my subscripts have to be whole numbers, so I divide each of these by the smallest common factor, 1.15:

I end up with an empirical formula of NaSO2. (If a subscript is 1, it’s not shown.)

I can then calculate a mass for the empirical formula by adding together the atomic masses on the periodic table of 1 sodium.
```



### Understanding the Role of Moles in Chemical Reactions

A chemist takes certain things called reactants and makes something new from them. A cook does the same thing with ingredients. Both follow a set of directions: the cook follows a recipe while the chemist follows the balanced chemical reaction. Both can scale up or down to produce more or less product.

#### Making the Calculations

If you know the formula weight of the reactants and product, you can calculate how much you need and how much you’ll get. For example, consider the Haber reaction, which is a method of preparing ammonia ($\text{NH}_3$) by reacting nitrogen gas ($\text{N}_2$) with hydrogen gas ($\text{H}_2$):

\[
\text{N}_2(g) + 3\text{H}_2(g) \rightarrow 2\text{NH}_3(g)
\]

In this reaction, one mole of nitrogen gas reacts with three moles of hydrogen gas to produce two moles of ammonia. The molar masses are approximately:

- Nitrogen ($\text{N}$): 14.01 g/mol
- Hydrogen ($\text{H}$): 1.008 g/mol
- Ammonia ($\text{NH}_3$): 17.03 g/mol

Calculating the amounts needed for a reaction can be done using these molar masses. For example, to produce 34.06 grams of ammonia, you would need:

\[
\text{Moles of } \text{NH}_3 = \frac{34.06 \text{ g}}{17.03 \text{ g/mol}} \approx 2 \text{ moles}
\]

From the balanced equation, you can determine the moles of nitrogen and hydrogen required:

- For nitrogen:
\[
\text{Moles of } \text{N}_2 = \frac{1}{2} \times 2 \text{ moles} = 1 \text{ mole}
\]

- For hydrogen:
\[
\text{Moles of } \text{H}_2 = 3 \times 1 \text{ mole} = 3 \text{ moles}
\]

Now, converting moles back to grams using the molar masses:

- For nitrogen:
\[
\text{Mass of } \text{N}_2 = 1 \text{ mole} \times 28.02 \text{ g/mol} = 28.02 \text{ g}
\]

- For hydrogen:
\[
\text{Mass of } \text{H}_2 = 3 \text{ moles} \times 2.016 \text{ g/mol} = 6.048 \text{ g}
\]

Thus, to produce 34.06 grams of ammonia, you would need approximately 28.02 grams of nitrogen and 6.048 grams of hydrogen.



```markdown
The balanced chemical reaction for the synthesis of ammonia is:

$$
\text{N}_2(g) + 3 \text{H}_2(g) \rightarrow 2 \text{NH}_3(g)
$$

To calculate the molecular weights of each reactant and product, use the periodic table to find the weights of the atoms:

- For nitrogen gas ($\text{N}_2$):
- Molecular weight = $2 \times 14.007 \, \text{g/mol} = 28.014 \, \text{g/mol}$

- For hydrogen gas ($\text{H}_2$):
- Molecular weight = $2 \times 1.008 \, \text{g/mol} = 2.016 \, \text{g/mol}$

- For ammonia ($\text{NH}_3$):
```
